# Preface.

<image-desc>A decorative headpiece composed of three horizontal rows of ornate woodcut ornaments, including floral patterns and interlocking geometric flourishes.</image-desc>

One is sufficiently assured that there already exist far too many books dealing with <term>Hermetic philosophy</term> <note>A tradition of esoteric knowledge named after Hermes Trismegistus, focusing here on the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note>; and that, since no one wished to write with total clarity—without ambiguous meanings and without allegory—concerning this science (which, indeed, no wise man will ever do), it would be better to remain silent altogether rather than to fill the world with new writings. Such works serve only to further confuse the minds of those who strive to fathom the <term>Philosophical secrets</term> <note>In the 17th and 18th centuries, "philosophy" often referred specifically to alchemy and the study of nature's hidden laws.</note>, rather than leading them onto the right path that would bring them to the desired place they intend to go.

For this reason, it has been believed that the translation and explanation of a good author, who deals emphatically with this high philosophy, would be far more beneficial to the <term>Sons of Science</term> <note>original: "Söhnen der Wissenschaft"; a traditional term for initiates or dedicated students of alchemy.</note> than some new parabolic presentation, adorned with the most ingenious speeches. Such displays are what the possessors of the Art are accustomed to devise when they treat this great Art—or rather, when they write books only for those who [lack] the mastery... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at "ent," likely intended to be "entbehren" (to lack) or "entzogen" (withdrawn).</note>

<summary>This preface justifies the publication as a translation of a trusted authority, arguing that original alchemical writings are often intentionally obscure and confusing to students.</summary>
<keywords>Hermetic philosophy, alchemy, Philosophical secrets, Sons of Science, translation, allegory, esoteric education</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, which argued that many alchemical authors wrote more to boast of their own success than to actually teach the "sacred science" to those who lack the mastery.</meta>

# Preface.

neither possess it as they do, nor indeed seek to suggest that they have had the good fortune to come into its possession. It is certain that most philosophers who have written on the subject have done so more to mention the successful outcome with which **God** has blessed their labor, than to instruct those who devote themselves to this sacred science <note>In this context, alchemy is referred to as a "sacred science" because it was seen as uncovering the hidden laws of God's creation.</note>, as would indeed be necessary. Truly, this is so certain that it was their primary intention when they composed books about it.

The little treatise that bears the title of *The Ancient War of the Knights* <note>original: "Uralten Ritter-Krieges"; a famous alchemical allegory, first appearing in the early 17th century, which describes the chemical process as a battle between metallic principles.</note> has, without a single contradiction, deserved to be accepted and approved by all the wise, as well as by those who have some knowledge of <term>Hermetic philosophy</term> <note>The esoteric tradition of alchemy and spiritual transformation named after the mythical figure Hermes Trismegistus.</note>. It is composed in the form of a dialogue, in a simple and natural manner that bears the hallmark of truth throughout. But for all its simplicity, does it not also possess deep understanding, power in its speech, and conviction in its proofs? Indeed, there is not a single word in it that does not contain an entire conclusion or final judgment, and upon which one would not have ample material to write a long <term>commentary</term> <note>original: "Commentarium"; a systematic series of explanations on a text.</note>. This work was composed in the German language by a true philosopher <note>original: "Philosopho"; in this era, "philosopher" was a common synonym for a practicing alchemist.</note> whose name is unknown. It was published in the year 1604 in Leipzig; Faber in Montpellier <note>Pierre-Jean Fabre (1588–1658), a famous French physician and alchemist known for his works defending the chemical medicine of Paracelsus.</note> translated it into Latin, from which Latin the French—

<keywords>The Ancient War of the Knights, Hermetic philosophy, alchemical history, 1604, Leipzig, Pierre-Jean Fabre, alchemical instruction</keywords>

# Preface.

<sig>A 3</sig>

—erman tongue. A French translation was prepared, which was printed in **Paris** by **d'Houry** <note>Laurent d'Houry (1644–1725) was a prominent French printer and bookseller known for publishing scientific and medical texts.</note>, and was appended to the end of those writings titled *The French Turba* <note>original: "La Tourbe Françoise"; refers to a French edition of the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest and most important Latin alchemical texts, which takes the form of a debate among pre-Socratic philosophers.</note>: *The Abandoned Word* <note>original: "La parole delaissée"; an alchemical treatise traditionally attributed to Bernard of Trevisan.</note> and the works of **Drebbel** <note>original: "Drebelius"; referring to Cornelis Drebbel (1572–1633), a Dutch inventor and alchemist who famously built an early submarine and studied the nature of air.</note>, which together comprise a single volume. However, the aforementioned **Faber** <note>likely Pierre-Jean Fabre, a 17th-century French physician and alchemist.</note> either did not properly understand the German, or he intentionally corrupted the original, with the result that certain corrupted passages are found in these two translations. The falsity of these errors is so manifest that it has brought this little work into contempt among many people, even though it otherwise appeared to be of very great value.

Just as truth and falsehood in a single *subject* <note>original: "Subjecto"; here referring to the underlying matter or topic of a logical argument.</note> agree very poorly with one another, it was easy to conclude that the translation must not have been handled faithfully and sincerely. Thus, a *philosopher* <note>original: "Philosophus"</note> of exceptional knowledge and *extraordinary merits* <note>original: "extraordinairen meriten"</note> was found who, to satisfy his own *curiosity* <note>original: "curiosität"</note> regarding this matter, took the trouble to search for the German *copy* <note>original: "Exemplar"</note> of this little treatise for more than ten years. After he finally discovered it, he had it translated into *Latin* with exact precision. From this copy, this new translation into French has now been made with the greatest possible faithfulness and sincerity. In this version, one will recognize the original as sound because of the truth that shines through, now that many passages have been corrected which were previously not only changed, but—

<meta>catchword: but rather (original: dern, completing sondern)</meta>

<summary>The author critiques previous French translations of "The Ancient War of the Knights," specifically one published by d'Houry, for being inaccurate or intentionally falsified. He explains that a dedicated scholar spent ten years locating the original German text to produce a new, more faithful Latin and French version.</summary>

<keywords>translation history, d'Houry, The French Turba, Cornelis Drebbel, Pierre-Jean Fabre, textual integrity, Latin translation, manuscript recovery</keywords>

# Preface.

—nly, but also entirely reversed. One can conclude this from number 34, where the first translation reads just like Faber’s Latin: *“No one can attain our mercury; unless from eight soft bodies, nor can any be prepared without the other”* <note>original: "Mercurium nostrum nemo assequi potest; nisi ex mollibus octo corporibus, neque ullum absque altero parari potest." In alchemical theory, the number of "bodies" or substances required was a point of great technical importance; a mistranslation of "one" as "eight" would fundamentally change the recipe.</note>. This was already enough to bring this writing into contempt among those who have enough knowledge of the *principles* <note>original: "principiorum"</note> of the work to distinguish the true from the false. Nevertheless, the wise could easily deduce that such a fundamental and major error as this could not have come from any true *philosopher* <note>original: "Philosopho"; in this context, "Philosopher" refers to a master of alchemy or the Hermetic arts</note>, who otherwise makes it sufficiently clear that he perfectly understands the mastery. 

But here it required such a wise man who was zealous for the discovery of the truth and was in such a position that he could undertake such a sharp investigation into the original of this work; without this, it would have been impossible to restore the missing correct understanding and meaning here.

The passage just mentioned is not the only one that had to be set right. If one takes the trouble to compare the new translation against the previous one, one will find a great difference and many significant *corrections* <note>original: "correctiones"</note>, among which number 35 is not the least. And because this translation was taken from the new Latin copy, without regard for the one that had been printed in French before, everything was thereafter [compared] with

<meta>catchword: sonder-</meta>

<summary>The author explains how a major error in previous editions—claiming eight "soft bodies" are needed for Mercury—led experts to doubt the work's authenticity. He justifies the need for this new translation based on a superior Latin manuscript to restore the true alchemical meaning.</summary>

# Preface.

->A 4<-

...noted with particular pleasure what was found not to be uniform with the first. The words and entire phrases <note>original: "Phrases"</note> added in several places to facilitate an easier connection or to achieve a more perfect sense <note>original: "Sensum"</note> are enclosed within two parentheses ( ), so that one can distinguish what belongs to the text from what does not. The translator <note>original: "Uebersezer"</note> has bound himself firmly and carefully to this text, since the slightest addition in a matter of such a nature can effect a highly questionable change and cause great errors.

The excellence and importance of this writing were well worth the effort of creating a commentary <note>original: "Commentarium"</note> on it, which would make a book more understandable to the Children of Science <term>Children of Science: A historical term for students of alchemy and the hermetic arts, implying they are the spiritual heirs to ancient wisdom</term>, a book that can serve them in place of all others.

And because the method of teaching through dialogues is most useful for instruction, making truths that are otherwise too lofty for some understandable and tangible, this method has been employed here—all the more so because the author, upon whom the commentary was made, also wrote in just such a manner. In the dialogue of Eudoxus and Pyrophilus <note>Eudoxus and Pyrophilus are classic persona in alchemical literature; Eudoxus represents the wise, experienced teacher, while Pyrophilus (the "lover of fire") is the dedicated student</note>, which explains the conversation of the Stone <note>Referring to the Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of the alchemical work</note> with Gold and Mercury <note>original: "Mercurio"</note>, one will find the principal and most prominent difficulties <note>original: "Difficultäten"</note> eased through the questions and answers that occur therein.

# Preface.

->A 4<-

<meta>catchword: learn-</meta>

concerning the principal points of Hermetic <term>Hermetic philosophy: A tradition of knowledge based on the writings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, focusing on alchemy, astrology, and theurgy</term> philosophy.

The marginal numbers of these two dialogues indicate how the passages of the first dialogue relate to those of the second, where they are explained. One will notice in this work a complete uniformity of opinions with the first masters of this philosophy, as well as with the wisest men who have written in recent centuries <note>original: "Sæculis"</note>; so that no treatise <note>original: "Tractat"</note> on this subject will be found, however many there may be, that is clearer and more sincere; and which consequently is more useful to those who apply themselves to the study of this science, and who otherwise possess all the good qualities of mind and spirit that our philosophy requires of those who desire to advance within it, than this very writing.

The commentary <note>original: "Commentarius"</note> will undoubtedly be found to be all the better for not being long-winded, as almost all commentaries <note>original: "Commentarii"</note> are; since it only touches upon those areas that might require some interpretation, and in no way distances itself from the subject <note>original: "Subjecto"</note>; however, since this type of writing is not for those who have as yet no beginning in this secret philosophy: so the most keen-sighted will surely notice that many things which perhaps deserved an interpretation were passed over, rather than attempting to explain everything in general, which to those learn-

<summary>This preface claims the text is highly consistent with ancient alchemical masters and argues that its concise commentary is superior to more long-winded interpretations. It targets readers who already possess some foundation in the secret philosophy and the necessary character for the work.</summary>

# Preface.

-ers <note>continuing from "learn-" on the previous page</note> of this great art might still encounter some difficulties.

Because the first of these dialogues recounts the victory of the Stone, and the other explains the causes and shows the foundation of its triumph: it seems this book could be published under no title more fitting than that of the **Hermetic Triumph**, or the victorious **Philosophical Stone** <term>Philosophical Stone: The legendary substance in alchemy capable of turning base metals into gold and achieving physical/spiritual perfection</term>. There is nothing further to report here, except that the author of the translation—who is also at the same time the author of the **Commentary** <note>original: "Commentarii"</note> and of the letter attached at the end of this book—has no other **interest** and no other intention in this than to reveal the truth to those who long for its knowledge, moved by the same reasons that belong to the true children of the science; furthermore, he declares and sincerely maintains that he desires with his whole heart that those who are unfortunate enough to waste their time laboring on foreign and distant materials <note>This refers to the "sophisters" who used the wrong substances—like common lead, salts, or organic waste—rather than the true starting material of the Great Work</note> might be enlightened by reading this book to recognize the true and only **Matter of the Wise**; and that those who have already recognized it, but do not know the important point of the dissolution of the stone and the coagulation of the water—and of the spirit and the body—might learn these secret **operations** <note>original: "operationes"</note> here; which are then described here clearly enough for them.

# Preface.

The Author did not find it feasible to write in Latin, as he is not of the opinion, like many others, that the secrets are dishonored if one treats them in the mother tongue <note>The author is defending his choice to write in German rather than Latin, which was the standard language for "serious" academic and alchemical works to prevent the "unworthy" from reading them</note>. In this, he has followed the example of many philosophers who wished for their work to bear the character of their fatherland. Thus, his primary purpose has also been to be useful to all his countrymen; doubting not that, if this treatise <term>treatise: a formal written work on a subject</term> should seem of any worth to the students of Hermes <note>original: "Schülern Hermetis"; refers to practitioners of alchemy, who consider themselves followers of the legendary Hermes Trismegistus</note>, there will surely be some found who

->will translate it into a<-
->language that pleases them, etc.<-

### A General
## Drafted Explanation
### of the
# Copperplate Engraving
### or
## Emblematic Image.

One should not expect here an explanation, piece by piece, that would entirely pull aside the curtain hanging over this philosophical riddle <note>original: "Philosophischen Räthsel"; refers to the complex symbolic puzzles common in alchemical literature</note>, so that the truth might be presented completely naked and bare. If this were the case, then one might as well throw all the writings of the philosophers into the fire at once. The wise would have no more advantage over the ignorant; both parties would be equally skilled, one as much as the other, in this wondrous art <term>wondrous art: a traditional name for alchemy or the "Great Work"</term>.

One will, therefore, be content to see in this figure—as if in a mirror—the brief summary of the entire secret philosophy which is contained in this little book, wherein all parts of this emblem <note>original: "Sinn-Bildes"; an allegorical image meant to convey spiritual or physical truths through symbols</note> are to be found explained as clearly as has been permitted.

<meta>catchword: Die (The)</meta>

<keywords>allegory, emblem, philosophical riddle, copperplate engraving, secret philosophy, wondrous art, alchemy, interpretation</keywords>

# Explanation

Those who have already made a beginning in the **Philosophical Secrets** <note>original: "Philosophischen Geheimniſſen"; refers to the study of alchemy and hermetic philosophy</note> will immediately notice what understanding and meaning is hidden beneath this figure. But those who do not yet possess such light should generally consider here how Heaven and Earth have, as it were, a secret understanding with each other and agree with one another through the mediation of the **Sun** and the **Moon**, which are like the secret bonds of this philosophical union <term>philosophical union: the "Chemical Wedding" or the joining of opposite principles to create the Philosophers' Stone</term>.

In the labor of this work, you will see **two parabolic streams** <note>original: "paraboliſche Bäche"; likely referring to the two main liquid components or paths of the alchemical process</note> which, flowing secretly together, conceive and bring forth the **mysterious triangular stone** with one another, which is the foundation of this art.

You will see a **secret and natural fire**, whose spirit—in the moment it penetrates the stone—drives it upward into vapors or **sublimates** <term>sublimates: the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then collects again as a solid</term> it, so that the vapors become thick within the vessel.

You will see what power and effect the **sublimated stone** receives from the Sun and Moon, which are its father and mother <note>a reference to the "Emerald Tablet," a foundational alchemical text stating "The Sun is its father, the moon its mother"</note>, whose first **Crown of Perfection** it inherits from them right from the beginning.

You will see, in the continuation of the work: That the **Art** gives this **Divine Juice** or liquid <note>original: "Göttlichen Safte oder liquori"</note> a double Crown of Perfection through the reversal or **conversion of the elements**, and through the extraction and purification of the beginnings <note>original: "Anfänge"; referring to the Three Principles of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury</note>, whereby it becomes the mystery-rich **herald’s staff of Mercury** <note>original: "Herolds-Stab des Mercurii"; the Caduceus, a symbol of the balanced union of opposing forces</note>,

<meta>Catchword: becomes,</meta>

<summary>This page describes the primary symbols of the alchemical process—the Sun and Moon, the triangular stone, and the staff of Mercury—explaining how they interact through fire and sublimation to reach perfection.</summary>
<keywords>Sun and Moon, triangular stone, sublimation, conversion of elements, Caduceus, Mercury, alchemical fire, philosophical secrets</keywords>

# of the Copperplate Engraving.

...is, which works such wonder-worthy transformations.

You will see that this very **Mercury** <term>Mercury: represented by the symbol ☿, this is "Philosophical Mercury," the fluid, transformative principle in alchemy</term>, like a **Phoenix**, is born anew in the fire; through mastery, it reaches the final perfection of the **Fixed Sulfur of the Sages** <term>Fixed Sulfur: the stable, non-volatile principle that gives form and "fixity" to the alchemical stone</term>. This sulfur imparts to it the highest power over the **Three Realms of Nature** <note>Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral kingdoms</note>, of which the **Triple Crown** (for which reason the **Hieroglyph of the World** <note>likely the *globus cruciger* or orb and cross symbol</note> was placed upon it) is the most essential sign.

You will finally see in its proper place what the part of the celestial **Zodiac** <note>original: "Thier-Kreyses"</note> with the three signs presented there signifies. Thus, when all these interpretations are held together, it will not be impossible to extract the complete understanding of the whole **Secret Philosophy** <note>original: "geheimen Philosophiæ"</note> and the greatest part of the **Work** <note>the *Great Work* or *Magnum Opus* of alchemy</note>, which has been sufficiently detailed at length in the **Epistle** <note>original: "Send-Schreiben"</note> to the true **Disciples of Hermes** <note>original: "Hermetis"; referring to followers of Hermes Trismegistus</note>, which is appended to the end of this work, etc.

# DISPUTATION
## of Gold and Mercury
->with<-
## the Philosophers' Stone.

A true philosopher <note>original: "Philosophus"; in alchemical texts, this refers to an "adept" or one who has mastered the secrets of nature.</note> writes thus: By almighty **God**, and by the salvation of my soul, I declare this to you, seekers of this glorious art, from a faithful heart and out of compassion for those who have long been searching: that our (*) whole work arises from only one thing, <term>one thing: a central alchemical tenet (the *Materia Prima*) suggesting that all stages of the Great Work derive from a single, universal substance.</term> and is completed within itself, and requires nothing more
<margin>1.</margin>
than (*) dissolution and re-hardening. <term>dissolution and re-hardening: a translation of the Latin *solve et coagula*, the process of breaking a substance down to its core and rebuilding it in a perfected state.</term> And this must happen of its own accord, without any foreign substances.
<margin>2.</margin>
Just as **Ice**, which is placed in a dry vessel over the fire and is turned into water by the
<margin>3.</margin>
warmth: (*) So it is also with our Stone, which needs nothing more than the help of the artist’s manual labor and
<margin>4.</margin>
the natural (*) fire. <note>natural fire: often refers to a gentle, sustained heat compared to the warmth of a brooding hen, rather than a violent flame.</note> For from it

## Discourse of the PHILOSOPHERS' STONE
### with GOLD & MERCURY.

The subject of this discourse is a dispute that Gold and Mercury one day had with the Philosophers' Stone. Here is the manner in which a true Philosopher <note>original: "Philosophe"; in alchemical literature, this refers to an "adept" or one who has mastered the secrets of nature’s laws.</note> (who has attained the possession of this great secret) speaks.

I protest to you before God, and upon the (eternal) salvation of my soul, with a sincere heart, touched by compassion for those who have long been engaged in great research; and (I certify) to all of you who cherish this marvelous art, that our whole work takes birth (*) from a single <margin>1.</margin> thing, <term>single thing: a reference to the "Materia Prima," the universal starting material that alchemists believed could be transformed into any substance.</term> and that in this thing the work finds its perfection, without needing anything else, other than being dissolved (*) and coagulated, <margin>2.</margin> <term>dissolved and coagulated: the central alchemical process of *solve et coagula*, breaking down a substance to its essence and then rebuilding it in a pure form.</term> which it must do of itself, without the help of any foreign thing.

When one puts ice in a vessel placed over the fire, one sees that the heat causes it to resolve into water: (*) one must act in the same manner with <margin>3.</margin> our stone, which needs nothing but the help of the artist, the operation of his hands, and the action of the natural (*) fire: <note>natural fire: alchemists often distinguished between "common fire" (literal flames) and "natural fire" (a steady, gentle heat, sometimes compared to the internal heat of the earth or a brooding hen).</note> for it will not resolve <margin>4.</margin>

<summary>This page introduces an alchemical dialogue between Gold, Mercury, and the Philosophers' Stone, asserting that the "Great Work" requires only one primary substance and the application of natural heat.</summary>

It [the ore] cannot do this on its own, even if it were to lie in the earth forever; therefore, one must assist it, but not in such a way that one tries to add foreign and contrary things to it. Rather, just as God gives us the grain in the field, and we must mill and bake it so that it becomes bread: <margin>5.</margin> so it is here as well; (*) God has created this ore for us so that we might take it, destroy its coarse body, pick out the good from within, cast away the excess, and from the poison, create a medicine <note>original: "Arzney"; in alchemical contexts, this refers to the "Universal Medicine" or Elixir, capable of curing both diseased metals and human illness.</note>.

And so that you may understand it all the better, I wish to set down for you a fine conversation and dispute that took place between the Philosopher’s Stone, Gold, and Mercury <note>original: "Mercurio"; here referring to the personified chemical element or the "Philosophical Mercury" which alchemists believed was the soul of all metals.</note>. Through this, those who have long been searching and who know something of how to work with metals and <margin>6.</margin> minerals (*) may easily arrive at the true foundation. And it will be necessary that one learns to properly recognize every <margin>7.</margin> thing within the earth (*) both outwardly and inwardly, and what it is capable of in the depths of nature.

Now, Gold and Mercury once came upon a Stone with armed hands, intending to overcome it.

Gold, driven by fury, began to speak in this manner:

## Gold:

How do you have the audacity to raise yourself above me and my brother Mercury <note>original: "Mercure"</note>, and to claim preference over us; you, who are nothing but a <margin>8.</margin> worm swollen <note>original: "bouffi"; meaning puffed up or inflated</note> with venom? ## The Stone:

Dear Gold, it is true, God has granted you honor, beauty, and constancy <term>constancy (German: *Beständigkeit*); in alchemy, this refers to "fixity," or the ability of a metal to remain unchanged and non-volatile when exposed to intense heat</term>, and for such things you should rightly be grateful to God, and not despise others alongside you. Goldsmiths, or other such masters, when they wish to gild something, cannot do without Gold and me, Mercury; instead, they immediately bring us together and unite us without difficulty and with little effort. ## Our Stone.

Then our Stone spoke again with a laughing spirit: "Truly, you are both well worth laughing at for your 'proof,' and I marvel at you, especially Gold—since you boast so highly of many things and yet have no more understanding than this: Do you think that the ancient Philosophers <note>original: "Philosophi"</note> in their writings wrote merely according to common understanding, and intended to be understood exactly according to those very words?"

## Gold.

"These cited masters have written no untruth, and all agree with one another regarding my virtue; but there have certainly been some who have sought my virtues <term>virtutes: the inherent powers or medicinal properties believed to reside within a substance.</term> and power in other unseemly things, such as: in various herbs, animals, blood, urine, hair, seed <term>spermate: from the Latin 'sperma,' referring here to biological reproductive fluids or 'the seed of life' used in misguided experiments.</term>, and the like, and for that reason have surely failed,"

## The Stone.

They prove this themselves when they say: Where we write most clearly according to common understanding, there we have most hidden the Art. <margin>17.</margin> But (*) where we speak through figures, parables, and proverbs, there we have truly brought the Art to light; and where they write of gold or mercury <note>original: "Mercurio"</note>, they soon mention thereafter, and explain themselves, saying: That their gold is not common gold, and their mercury is not common mercury. For gold, because of its perfection, can never be further changed, because it has already become a completely perfect metal; and even if one were to extract its color a hundred times, working with it in an artful <term>artful: Here, "künstlich" refers to the skilled application of the Alchemical Art</term> way, it can and may do no more, nor color or tincture <term>tincture: A potent substance or "medicine" in alchemy capable of transforming or "dyeing" base metals into noble ones</term>, than the color and tincture it already possesses within itself. Therefore <margin>18.</margin> the philosophers <note>original: "Philosophi"</note> write: (*) One should seek in imperfect things, and thus one will find perfection within them, as you may read in the *Rosarium* <note>original: "Rosario"; likely the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a foundational 16th-century alchemical text</note>. Likewise writes Raymond Lully <note>original: "Raymundus Lullius"; a 13th-century polymath to whom many influential alchemical works were attributed</note>, whom you yourself have called as a witness, thus: That which is to become better must not be perfect; in perfect things, nothing changes—rather, it would sooner perish entirely.

## Gold.

I know well that they write such things, but that may be understood as referring to my brother *Mercury* <note>original: "Mercurio"</note>; he is still imperfect, and if we both mix together, he is made perfect by me, for he is of the female and I am of the male gender. Therefore the *Philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophi"</note> say: The Art is entirely a *homogeneous thing* <note>original: "homogeneum"; a substance consisting of parts of the same kind</note>. For this reason, you can see clearly among human beings that no child can be born without man and woman, but only through the mixing of them both. The same is also to be seen in all animals that have life.

## Gold.

To show you that I have spoken the truth, and in order to defend my honor, I wish to rely only on the authority of those same masters whom you have cited to me, and whom, consequently, you have no right to reject. Let us look particularly at Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundational texts of alchemy are attributed.</note>. He speaks thus: "It is true, without lie, certain, and most true: that which is below is like that which is above, and that which is above is like that which is below (*)" <margin>25.</margin> "it is by these things that the miracles of a single thing can be performed." <note>This is a translation of the opening lines of the *Tabula Smaragdina* (Emerald Tablet), the most famous and foundational text in Western alchemy.</note>

This is how Aristotle <note>While the historical Aristotle did not write alchemical texts, many medieval works on the subject were attributed to him (known as Pseudo-Aristotle) and were considered authoritative by alchemists.</note> speaks: "Oh, how admirable is this thing, which contains within itself all the things we need. It kills itself, and then it recovers life from itself; (*) it marries itself, it <margin>26.</margin> conceives by itself; it is born of itself; it dissolves itself in its own blood; it coagulates again with it and takes on a hard consistency; it becomes white; it becomes red by itself; we add nothing more to it, and we change nothing in it, except that we separate the coarseness and the earthiness from it." <note>This passage describes the "hermetic marriage" and the self-contained nature of the "First Matter," which contains its own opposites (male/female, fixed/volatile) and requires only purification rather than the addition of external substances.</note>

The philosopher Plato speaks of me in these terms: "It is one single, unique thing, of one and the same species in itself; (*) it has a body, <margin>27.</margin> a soul, a spirit, and the four elements <note>The traditional elements of Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note>, upon

## Gold:

For although the Philosophers <note>In alchemical texts, "The Philosophers" refers to the ancient authorities and masters of the "Hermetic Art," such as Hermes Trismegistus.</note> speak of a single thing which contains within itself the four elements <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water—the building blocks of all matter in pre-modern science.</note>; which has a body, a soul, and a spirit; and though by this "thing" they mean to indicate the physical tincture <term>tincture</term> <note>The "tincture" is the concentrated essence or "medicine" capable of "dyeing" or transforming base metals into noble ones.</note> when it has been driven to its final perfection (which is the goal toward which they strive); nevertheless, this thing must, from its very beginning, be composed of me—who am Gold—and of my brother, who is Mercury <note>original: *le Mercure*</note>. At this, the **Gold** became angry and knew not how else to answer, but took counsel with his brother **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio"; the liquid metal personified as the messenger of the gods, representing the volatile or "fluid" principle in the work.</note>, and they reached an agreement: they would both help each other, because there were two of them while our **Stone** was only one. <margin>38.</margin> And when the battle began, our **Stone** released power from himself, (*) and destroyed and overcame them both; he swallowed them up, so that nothing at all could be seen of them, nor where they had gone. <note>This "swallowing" is a classic alchemical motif for the "Universal Solvent" or the process of "fixation," where the Stone transmutes and incorporates base or imperfect metals into its own perfect, unified nature.</note>

...so long as I can give all of you perfection, and raise you above the state in which nature placed you.

At these last words, the **Gold** <note>original: "l'or"</note> flew into a furious rage, no longer knowing how to respond. He held counsel with his brother **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercure"</note>, and they agreed together that they would assist one another, hoping that being two against our **Stone** <note>original: "pierre"</note>, which is but one and alone, they would easily overcome it. Thus, after being unable to defeat it through dispute, they resolved to put it to death by the sword. With this design, they joined their forces in order to increase them by the union of their double power.

The combat took place. Our **Stone** deployed its strength and its valor: it fought them both; (*) overcame them; scattered them; and swallowed them both up; <margin>38.</margin> so that no trace remained that could reveal what had become of them. <note>In alchemical allegory, the "Stone" (the Elixir or Tincture) "swallowing" Gold and Mercury represents the process of <term>projection</term> or <term>fixation</term>, where the Stone absorbs the properties of other metals to achieve a perfected, unified state.</note>

Thus, dear friends, you who have the fear of **God** <note>original: "Dieu"</note> before your eyes, what I have just told you should make you know the truth, and enlighten your mind as much as is necessary to understand the foundation of the greatest and most precious of all treasures, which no **Philosopher** <note>original: "Philosophe"; historically referring to an alchemist or "Philosopher by Fire"</note> has ever so clearly explained, discovered, or brought to light.

# * ( o ) *

Therefore, nothing further is required of you, except that you pray to **GOD** <note>original: "GOTT"</note> so that He may grant you the favor of becoming a partaker of such a precious **jewel** <note>original: "Kleinod"; a term used here to describe the Philosopher's Stone as the ultimate spiritual and physical treasure</note>. After this, sharpen your reason, pique your senses, read with understanding, work diligently, and do not rush this <margin>39.</margin> precious work. For (*) it must have its own natural time to ripen, just like fruit upon a tree or grapes upon a vine <note>Alchemical writers frequently emphasized that the "Great Work" could not be forced; it followed a natural, organic cycle of growth similar to agriculture</note>. And be of good will and purpose, otherwise **GOD** will grant you nothing; for **GOD** gives to those who desire to do good with it, <margin>40.</margin> (*) and takes it from those—or withholds it from them—who wish to use it for evil. May **GOD** give you His blessing. Amen.

->**THE END.**<-

Nothing remains for you but to pray to **God** <note>original: "Dieu"</note>, that He might be pleased to bring you to the possession of a **jewel** <note>original: "joyau"; a common alchemical metaphor for the Philosopher's Stone</note>, which is of inestimable price. After this, sharpen the point of your minds: Read the writings of the **Sages** <note>The "Sages" refer to the historical alchemical authorities and "philosophers" whose cryptic texts the student must decipher</note> with prudence; work with diligence <insert>and exactitude</insert>; do not act with precipitation in a work so precious. <margin>39.</margin> (*) It has its time ordered by nature; just as the fruits that are upon the trees, and the clusters of grapes that the vine bears <note>This passage reinforces the "Hermetic" principle that alchemy is the "art of nature," requiring the same patient seasons as agriculture</note>. Have uprightness in your heart, and propose to yourself <insert>in your work</insert> an honest end; otherwise God will grant you nothing: <margin>40.</margin> (*) for He does not communicate <insert>so great</insert> a gift, except to those who wish to make good use of it; and He deprives those who have the intention of using it to commit evil. I pray God that He gives you His <insert>holy</insert> blessing.

The first is the *Subject* <note>original: "Subjectum"</note> of Philosophy considered in the state of the first *preparation* <note>original: "præparation"</note>, in which it is truly a stone, for it is solid, hard, heavy, brittle, and can be crushed: It is a *body* <note>original: "Corpus"</note> (says *Philaletha* <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, a 17th-century alchemist whose identity is often associated with George Starkey</note>) because it flows in the fire like a metal. It is just as if he were saying: Take the Stone of the Wise, which is the *matter* of the Philosophical Stone.

## Pyrophilus.

This is the very first point among those that have caused me some scruples <note>In this context, "scruples" refers to intellectual doubts or hesitations regarding a theory</note>; for it seems to me that there are reasons to doubt whether one should seek perfection in one single substance alone, and whether one can accomplish everything with it without the addition of any other thing. ## Eudoxus.

It is quite easy to free you from this doubt by means of this analogy: Just as the juices extracted from various herbs—once purified of their dregs <note>original: "Träbern," referring to the husks or solid residue left after pressing</note> or adhering impurities and incorporated with one another—are only a single preparation <note>original: "confection"</note> of one and the same species; so the Sages rightly call their prepared matter <term>Matter: The "Materia Prima" or raw starting substance which alchemists believe can be transformed into the Philosopher's Stone</term> a single and one-

-and-the-same thing; although one knows very well that it is a natural composition <note>original: "Compositum"</note> of several substances <note>original: "Substantiis"</note> from a single root, and from a single species <note>original: "Specie"</note>, which together make a complete whole, and a homogeneous thing <note>original: "homogeneum"</note>. In this sense, the Sages are all in agreement; although some say that their Matter <term>Matter: The "Materia," the physical substance the alchemist works upon to create the Philosopher's Stone</term> is composed of two things, others of three, some write that it is of four, even indeed of five, and finally others, that it is a single thing. They are all right at the same time, because unified things of a single species—just like many waters distilled and mixed from herbs nevertheless make no more than one single thing together—this also happens in our Art, and indeed with better reason, because the substances <note>original: "Substantiæ"</note> that come to the Philosophical Composition <note>original: "Philosophischen Composito"</note> are far less different from each other than sorrel-water <note>original: "Sauerrampf-Wasser"</note> is from lettuce-water <note>original: "Salat-Wasser"</note>.

<summary>Eudoxus explains that although various alchemists describe the "Matter" as being composed of multiple parts, it is fundamentally one substance because its components share a single "root" or species. ## Eudoxus.

Your observation is ingenious; the greatest part of the true Philosophers are of this very thought. The **Matter** requires nothing more than to be dissolved, and thereafter thickened and hardened; the mixing, the joining together, the **fixation** <term>Fixation: The process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat</term>, the **coagulation**, and other similar effects occur almost of themselves. But **dissolution** is the great mystery of the Art.

This is the **essential point** <note>original: "Essential-Punct"</note> which the Sages by no means reveal. All the labors of the first work, or the first **medicine** <term>Medicine: In alchemy, the "Arzney" or Elixir that "cures" base metals to turn them into gold, or restores human health</term>, are, properly speaking, nothing other than a continuous dissolution; in such a manner that the **calcination**, **extraction**,

<meta>catchword: Subli-</meta>

<summary>Eudoxus explains that "dissolution"—turning a solid into a liquid state—is the most important and guarded secret of alchemy. He notes that while other processes like fixation or coagulation happen almost naturally, the continuous dissolution of the Matter is the true work of the first stage.</summary>
<keywords>dissolution, hardening, Philosophical Matter, fixation, coagulation, calcination, extraction, First Work, alchemy secrets</keywords>

*Sublimation* <term>Sublimation: The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collecting the purified solid that forms upon cooling</term> and *Distillation* are nothing but a true *Dissolution* <note>original: "Solution"</note> of the *Matter*. *Geber* <note>Referring to the 8th-century alchemist Jabir ibn Hayyan, whose Latinized works were foundational to European alchemy</note> taught the *necessity* of *Sublimation* for no other reason than that it not only cleanses the *Matter* from its coarse and combustible parts; but also because it makes it fit for dissolution, from which the *mercurial moisture*, which is the **key of the work**, arises.

If you know the laws of the operation of the Work as well as you seem to me to grasp the **theory** <note>original Latin: *theoriam*</note> of it, you will have no need of my explanations at all.

## Pyrophilus.

I beg your pardon. I am still far from having reached as far as you imagine. That which you suppose to be the effect of a perfect **knowledge** <note>original German: *Erkänntniß*</note> of the Art in me is only a slight **expression** <note>original Latin/French-root: *expression*</note> which comes from nothing other than the reading of **authors** <note>original Latin: *auctorum*</note>, of whom my memory is full. On the contrary, I am quite near to despair [of reaching] such high...

### *Eudoxus.*

It is true that this **philosopher** sets neither goal nor end for the one who seeks the knowledge of this wonderful **art** <term>art: *Kunst* in German. In alchemical texts, "the Art" refers specifically to the practice of alchemy, implying a combination of physical labor and divine inspiration.</term>: For the sage should fully know nature in general, and the effects it carries out, both in the **center** <note>literally "mid-point"</note> of the earth, in the birth of **minerals** and **metals**; as well as upon the earth in the production of **plants** and **animals**. He shall also recognize the **universal matter** <note>original Latin: *universalem materiam*</note>, and the **particular and immediate matter** <note>original Latin: *materiam particularem & immediatam*</note> in which nature works for the **birth** <note>original German: *Gebährung*</note> of all beings; finally, he shall recognize the natural inclination and **sympathy** <note>original Latin: *Sympathiam*</note>, as well as the **antipathy** <note>original Latin: *antipathiam*</note> and natural aversion found between all things in the world. ## Pyrophilus.

## Eudoxus.

There is no doubt that gold possesses a great power within itself for the preservation of health and the curing of the most dangerous diseases. Copper, tin, lead, and iron are always used successfully by physicians, as is silver; because their dissolution or decomposition <note>original: *decomposition*</note>—which brings their properties to light—is much easier than that of gold. Therefore, the more the preparations <note>original: *præparationes*</note> that common artisans perform on these metals resemble the principles and the practice <note>original: *practicæ*</note> of our Art, the more they bring to light the wonderful virtue of gold.

But I tell you in truth, that without knowledge of our mastery <term>Mastery: The advanced knowledge and skill required to complete the "Great Work" of alchemy, specifically the ability to break down metals to their core essence.</term>, which alone teaches the essential destruction of gold, it is impossible to create the **universal medicine** <note>original Latin: *medicinam universalem*; a legendary substance capable of curing all illness and perfecting all matter</note>. Rather, the Wise can create it much more easily with the **Gold of the Sages** than with the **Gold of the Common People**. Thus you see that the author has the **Stone** reply to the Gold: that it should be angry with God for not granting it (the gold) the advantage

## Pyrophilus.

Only reflect upon the observations which I have asked you to note concerning the three different states of the Stone; then you will recognize as well as I that in the beginning he must be entirely **volatile**, and consequently entirely **fugitive**, so that he may be purified of all earthly coarseness and brought from imperfection to perfection, which grants him **mastery** <term>Mastery: The successful completion of a stage in the Great Work, allowing the alchemist to control the substance's properties.</term> in the other two states. ## Eudoxus.

You see clearly that the author says that the fluidity of the Stone serves the <term>artist</term> <note>original: *Artisten*; the practitioner or alchemist.</note> to his advantage; yet he adds at the same time that the artist must know the way and manner to extract the fluidity—that is, this moisture which causes this fluidity and is the only thing the Sage needs. Therefore, there is a good reason that it boasts of this with all the more right, because this fluidity does not at all prevent it from being endowed with an even more fixed soul than gold. ## Eudoxus.

There is a great difference between a positive declaration that this or that matter is not the true *subject* <term>subject</term> <note>original: *subjectum*; the underlying material or "first matter" required for the alchemical process.</note> of the Art—just as they make a declaration regarding *gold* and *mercury* <note>original: *Mercurio*</note>—and an instruction through figures and parables of the most essential metaphors, given to the children of science <note>A traditional term for initiated students of alchemy who are deemed worthy of understanding its secrets.</note>, who have the advantage of looking through and perceiving the philosophical truths in the midst of the enigmatic veils with which the sages know how to cover them. In the first case, when speaking negatively, the sages tell the truth without *equivocation* <term>equivocation</term> <note>original: *æquivocation*; the use of ambiguous language to hide the truth or mislead the uninitiated.</note>. ## Eudoxus.

Be very careful here, and consider well that, when the **Mercury of the Sages** <term>Mercury of the Sages</term> <note>The specialized, purified substance used as the "soul" or "seed" of the Great Work, distinct from common quicksilver.</note> has been elevated by the **Art** <note>The practice of alchemy.</note> from an imperfect state to a perfect one, this perfection is not of the same order as that where Nature stops working and producing her things upon reaching the **degree** <note>original: *Gradus*</note> of that perfection required by the **species** <note>original: *species*</note> of the produced thing—as is the case with the **Mercury of the Laypeople**. On the contrary, the perfection which the Art gives to the Mercury of the Sages is nothing other than a **middle state** <note>original: *Mittelstand*</note>, a **disposition** <note>original: *disposition*</note>, and a **potentiality** <note>original: *potentia*</note> that makes it fit to be brought, through the continuation of the **Work** <note>The *Magnum Opus* or Great Work of alchemy.</note>, into the state of **Super-perfection** <note>original: *Ubervollkommenheit*; a state of maturity beyond what nature can achieve alone.</note>. This state gives it the power, through the completion of the **Mastery** <note>original: *Meisterschaft*; the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, to afterward make the imperfect things perfect as well.

## Pyrophilus.

These reasons, as abstract as they are in themselves, do not fail to be understandable

### *Pyrophilus.*

It is a great gain to become wise from the misfortune of others. As for me, I will strive to learn from the errors of such people and to improve myself, following the good **Philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophis*; the established masters of the alchemical tradition.</note>, and conducting myself according to the light with which you are so kind as to guide me. One thing that causes much delusion among practitioners regarding the **Gold** and **Mercury of the common people** <note>original: *Gold und Mercurium der Layen*; "Layen" or "laity" refers to those who are not initiated into the secrets of alchemy and use ordinary, store-bought materials.</note> is, among others, the general claim of the Wise: namely, that their **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> is composed of man and woman, and that, in their opinion, **Gold** represents the role of the husband and **Mercury** that of the wife. ...please instruct me as to what this difference consists of.

## Eudoxus.

It is a certain truth that the union of man and woman is ordained by nature for the birth of animals and humans; but the union of man and woman required for the production of the **Elixir** <term>Elixir</term> <note>original: *Elixirs*; in alchemy, the "Elixir" or "Tincture" is the refined substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold or healing all illnesses.</note>, as well as of the metals, is to be understood entirely **allegorically** or by way of a parable. It is no more necessary than it is for the production of plants, whose seed alone holds within itself everything required for the sprouting, growth, and multiplication of the plants. Therefore, you should note well that the **Philosophical Matter** <note>original: *Philosophische Materia*; the raw material used to begin the Great Work.</note>, or the **Mercury of the Wise** <note>original: *Mercurius der Weisen*; not common quicksilver, but a specialized alchemical substance.</note>, is a true seed, which, although it is a **homogeneous substance** <note>original: *in seiner Substantia ein homogeneum*; a substance that is uniform and consistent throughout its physical form.</note> in its outward form, nevertheless does not cease to be of a dual nature. That is, it shares equally in the nature of **Metallic Sulfur** <note>original: *Metallischen Schwefels*; the active, "male," or fiery principle in alchemy.</note> as well as of **Metallic Mercury** <note>original: *Metallischen Mercurii*; the passive, "female," or watery principle.</note>, which are inwardly and inseparably united with one another. One of these takes the place of the man, and the other the place of the woman. For this reason, the Wise also call it a **hermaphrodite** <term>hermaphrodite</term> <note>original: *Zwitter*; literally a hybrid or crossbreed, used here to describe the "Rebis," a being that combines both male and female principles into one body.</note>; that is, it partakes of both the male and female sex at the same time:

You are not unaware of how much higher the **universal matter** <note>original: *universalis materia*; the primordial substance from which all things are created.</note> sits above the **particular** <note>original: *particulare*; specific, finished substances like common iron or lead.</note>. --- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 112. Pyrophilus is discussing the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, specifically focusing on a philological critique of how the Latin text was translated into French and interpreted by the commentator Hortulanus. The catchword "Eudo-" indicates that Eudoxus will reply on the following page.</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>

<header>* ( 6 ) *</header>

...prepared together of an unspeakable nature. But I do not know if you will be of my opinion regarding the interpretation <note>original: *Verdolmetschung*</note> of this saying and the commentary <note>original: *Commentarium*</note> of Hortulanus <note>Hortulanus, or "The Gardener," was a 14th-century alchemical author known for his influential commentary on the *Tabula Smaragdina* (Emerald Tablet).</note>. One reads shortly after these words: (That which is below is like that which is above, and that which is above is like that which is below). One reads, I say, following this: (In the French) "In order to perform the miracles of a single thing" <note>original: *Pour faire les miracles d'une seule chose*</note>. I for my part find that the Latin text (from which it was translated into French) conveys a completely different meaning. For the word *quibus* <note>Latin: "by which" or "with which."</note> (in German, "with which"), which connects the following words with those preceding them, signifies as much as: *through these things* (that is, through the union of these two natures <note>Refers to the celestial/volatile and terrestrial/fixed natures mentioned on the previous page.</note>) one performs the miracles of a single thing. The French word *pour* <note>French: "for" or "in order to."</note> (in German, "in order to"), which the translator and commentator uses, destroys the sense and reason <note>original: *sensum und rationem*</note> of a passage <note>original: *passus*</note> that is in and of itself quite correct and quite understandable. Tell me, if it pleases you, whether my observation also has a solid basis.

<sig>H</sig>
<meta>catchword: Eudo-</meta>

<summary>Pyrophilus critiques a French translation of the Emerald Tablet, arguing that the Latin "quibus" implies the union of superior and inferior natures is the means of alchemical miracles, rather than the goal.</summary>
<keywords>Emerald Tablet, Hermes Trismegistus, Hortulanus, translation critique, Latin vs French, alchemy, two natures</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Eudoxus and Pyrophilus from page 113. Having just heard Pyrophilus critique the French translation of the "Emerald Tablet," Eudoxus now responds, admitting he had overlooked the error because he usually studies the text in its original Latin.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>

<header>* ( 0 ) *</header>

## Eudoxus.

It is not only correct, but there is also much that depends upon it. I confess to you that I have not perceived it until now: in this, you make the common proverb doubtful, which says: namely, that the disciple is not above his master <note>A biblical reference to Matthew 10:24, suggesting Pyrophilus has surpassed his teacher in this specific observation.</note>. But because I have hitherto read the Emerald Tablet <note>original: *smaragdische Tafel*; the *Tabula Smaragdina*, a foundational cryptic text of alchemy.</note> more in Latin than in my mother tongue: so the deficiency of the translation and the commentary <note>original: *Commentarii*</note> could never cause me doubt on that account, as can happen with those who are accustomed to reading this summary <note>original: *summarium*</note> of the high Hermetic Philosophy <note>original: *Hermetischen Philosophiæ*</note> only in French. In short: the upper nature and the lower nature are not equal to each other for the purpose of performing miracles; rather, they are equal to each other because one can, through them, perform miracles of the one single thing <note>This "one thing" is the *Prima Materia* or the fundamental substance that alchemists sought to transform into the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. Therefore, as you see, I am entirely of your opinion.

## Pyrophilus.

I am quite glad that I made this remark; I was afraid you might not speak well of it to me; and I am certain of this, the children of science <term>Children of science: a traditional term for initiates or sincere students of alchemy and the hermetic arts.</term> will also be slightly grateful to me,

<meta>catchword: That,</meta>

<summary>Eudoxus agrees with Pyrophilus’s correction of the Emerald Tablet's translation, confirming that alchemical miracles are performed through the union of the upper and lower natures rather than for their sake. This dialogue emphasizes the necessity of returning to Latin sources to avoid the errors introduced by French translators and commentators like Hortulanus.</summary>

<sig>H 2</sig>

...that I have obtained an explanation from you on this matter, which will undoubtedly please the disciples of the great Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundation of alchemy is attributed.</note>. One must not doubt that the wise Aristotle perfectly understood the Great Art <term>The Great Art: A traditional name for Alchemy, specifically the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone.</term>. What he has written about it clearly proves it: thus also the Stone, in this *War of the Knights* <note>original: *Ritter Kriege*; likely referring to "The Ancient War of the Knights," a famous alchemical allegory attributed to Limojon de Saint-Didier.</note>, knows how to make good use of this great philosopher's authority through a maxim that contains its most singular and wondrous properties. Be so kind and tell me how you understand this: the thing marries itself and gives <margin>26.</margin> birth from itself?

->Eudoxus.<-

The Stone marries itself in that, in the first birth, nature performs it alone, alongside the assistance of Art <note>In alchemical philosophy, "Art" refers to the human intervention or laboratory work that accelerates or perfects natural processes.</note>, which creates the perfect union of the two substances <note>original: *zwo Substantiarum*</note> that give it its essence. From this, at the same time, arises the essential purification of the metallic sulfur <note>original: *Schwefel*</note> and metallic mercury <note>original: *Mercurii*</note>. This is such a natural marriage and union that the artist, who lends a hand by providing the proper preparations <note>original: *Dispositionum*; the necessary conditions or physical states of the materials.</note>, [needs] no demonstration through the rules of Art regarding this mat... <meta>The text cuts off mid-word, to be continued on the next page.</meta>

<summary>Pyrophilus cites Aristotle as an authority on alchemy and asks Eudoxus to explain the paradoxical phrase "the thing marries itself." Eudoxus begins explaining that this refers to the natural union and purification of sulfur and mercury assisted by the alchemist's art.</summary>

...could [speak]; indeed, he could not even properly grasp the secret of this union.

**The Stone impregnates itself.** This occurs when **Art** <note>In alchemical texts, "Art" refers to the human skill and laboratory techniques used to assist and accelerate the hidden processes of Nature.</note> continues to assist **Nature** through purely natural means, thereby placing the Stone in the disposition <term>disposition: the specific physical and spiritual state or "readiness" required for alchemical transformation.</term> that is proper and necessary for it to impregnate itself with the **astral seed** <note>original: *astralischen Saamen*; the "astral seed" was believed to be a celestial influence or life-force that descended from the stars to give growth and fertility to minerals.</note>, which makes it fertile and capable of multiplication within its own kind.

**The Stone gives birth from itself:** For after it has married and impregnated itself—while Art does nothing else but assist Nature through a constant warmth <note>This refers to the "athanor" or furnace, which must provide a steady, gentle heat comparable to an incubating bird.</note> required for birth—the Stone, I say, then takes on a new birth from itself, just as the **Phoenix** is reborn from its own ashes. It becomes a **Son of the Sun**, the **universal medicine** <note>original: *allgemeine Arzeney*; the Panacea, a substance capable of curing all diseases and perfecting "sick" or base metals.</note> for everything that has life, and the true **living gold of the sages**, which, through the continuation of Art's help and the service of the artist, attains in a short time its royal ornament and the supreme sovereignty over all its brothers <note>The "brothers" are the base metals (like lead or tin) which the Stone has the power to "rule" by transforming them into gold.</note>.

### Pyrophilus.

I understand quite well that under these **principles** <term>principles: the fundamental components or laws governing alchemical transformation.</term> one can easily grasp all those other properties that **Aristotle** <note>Medieval and Renaissance alchemists often attributed their theories to Aristotle (or "Pseudo-Aristotle") to grant their work scientific authority and link it to his theories on the elements.</note> attributes to the Stone, such as: **to kill itself**; **to come to life again of itself**; **to dissolve of itself in its own blood**, **to harden itself again within that same blood**, and finally, in a word, to acquire all the properties of the **Philosopher's Stone**. I also find, based on this report, no further difficulty in the saying of **Plato**. However, I still ask you to tell me what this ancient Philosopher—along with those who followed him in this—understands when he says that the Stone has a **Body, a Soul, and a Spirit**, <note>original: *Leib, eine Seele und einen Geist*; these refer to the "Tria Prima" or the three essentials of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury, which must be separated and reunited in the "Great Work."</note> "27. and that all things are solely from it, out of it, and in it."

### Eudoxus.

According to the natural order, **Plato** should have preceded **Aristotle**, who was his student, and who presumably learned from him the **secret philosophy** <note>original: *geheime Philosophiam*; refers to the esoteric knowledge of the hidden laws of nature and alchemy.</note>. Aristotle likely wished to present and establish himself before **Alexander the Great** as its perfect master and possessor, just as one can see from several kinds of writ-

<summary>Pyrophilus asks for an explanation of Plato's triune description of the Stone (Body, Soul, and Spirit), leading Eudoxus to discuss the transmission of alchemical secrets from Plato to his student Aristotle.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Plato, Philosopher's Stone, Body Soul and Spirit, secret philosophy, Alexander the Great, alchemical lineage</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 117, where Eudoxus explains that despite the chronological order of the Greek philosophers, both Plato and Aristotle agree on the fundamental nature of the Stone.</meta>

-> * (6) * <-

...[perfect] **Philosopher** <note>This completes the thought from the previous page regarding Aristotle's desire to appear as a master of secret philosophy before Alexander the Great.</note> can easily judge such things. But little depends on this order, and if you weigh the saying of **Plato** well alongside that of **Aristotle**, you will not find much difference in their content and meaning. Nonetheless, to satisfy the question you have asked, I will simply tell you that the **Stone** has a **Body**, because it is—as I have told you before—an entirely metallic **substance** <term>substantia: the underlying material essence or physical reality of a thing.</term> which gives it weight and heaviness. It has a **Soul**, which is the purest substance of the elements, in which its **fixity and permanence** <note>original: *Fix- und Verbleiblich-seyn*; in alchemy, "fixity" is the ability of a substance to remain stable and not evaporate when subjected to the heat of the fire.</note> reside. It has a **Spirit**, which effects the **union** of the Body and the Soul; it receives this Spirit specifically from the **influence of the stars**, and it is, so to speak, the **chariot of the tinctures** <note>original: *Wagen der Tincturen*; the "Spirit" (often associated with Mercury) acts as the vehicle that carries the transformative "tincture" or power into the material being transformed.</note>. 

You will also be able to grasp quite easily how **all things are from it, through it, and in it**; just as you have already seen that the **Stone** is not only the **primary matter** <note>original: *erste Materie*; the fundamental, formless building block from which all metals and minerals are derived.</note> of all beings contained within the mineral and metallic kingdoms, but also that it is united with the **universal matter** <note>original: *Materia universali*; the primordial substance from which the entire universe was created.</note> from which all things have taken their birth. And this is the basis of the final **attributes** <note>original: *attributorum*.</note> which Plato ascribes to the Stone.

<summary>Eudoxus explains the alchemical trinity of Body, Soul, and Spirit within the Philosopher's Stone, linking its physical and metaphysical properties to the teachings of Plato and Aristotle.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 117. Pyrophilus expresses doubt regarding the universal claims made about the Philosopher's Stone, specifically how it can be the source of success even in limited or "particular" alchemical experiments conducted by less experienced practitioners.</meta>

-> * ( o ) * <-

H 4

<meta>catchword: Pyro-</meta>

# Pyrophilus.

<margin>28.</margin>
Because I see that the **Stone** has not only claimed the **universal properties** <term>proprietates universales: the fundamental qualities and powers that apply to all of nature and the entire universe.</term> for itself, but also wishes to maintain that if some **Artists** <note>alchemists or practitioners of the "Hermetic Art."</note> have succeeded in certain **particularities** <term>particularibus: specific, limited experiments or partial transmutations that do not achieve the full "Great Work."</term>, all of this originates from it alone. I confess that I find it very difficult to grasp how such a thing can be.

# Eudoxus.

This philosopher explains it clearly enough nonetheless. He says that some **Artists**, who recognized the **Stone** only in an imperfect way and knew only a part of the **Work** <note>the *Magnum Opus* or "Great Work" of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, yet meanwhile, after they worked with the Stone and found the means to separate its **Spirit**—which holds its **Tincture** <term>tincture: the potent, concentrated essence or "dye" capable of transforming the inner nature of a substance.</term> within it—from it, they brought it so far that they added some of it to the **imperfect metals** <note>base metals like lead or tin, which alchemists believed were "immature" versions of gold.</note> which have an affinity with the Stone. But because they did not have a total and complete acquaintance with its **virtues** <note>original: *Tugenden*; the inherent powers, medicinal properties, or transformative strengths of a substance.</note>, nor the proper manner and way to conduct the work with it, their labor did not bring them much benefit; furthermore, the number of such Artists is truly very small.

<summary>Pyrophilus questions how the Philosopher's Stone can be responsible for partial alchemical successes, while Eudoxus explains that some alchemists achieve minor results by extracting the Stone's spirit without fully understanding its complete power.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, universal properties, particularities, alchemists, Tincture, imperfect metals, virtues, Great Work</keywords>

That it has a **Soul**, which is the purest **substance** of the elements, in which its **fixity** <term>fix- und verbleiblichseyn: the alchemical property of being "fixed," or stable and resistant to fire.</term> and permanence consists. ### Pyrophilus.

Because I see that the **Stone** has not only claimed the **universal properties** <note>original: *proprietates universales*</note> for itself, but also wishes to assert that, when several **artists** <note>original: *Artisten*; in this context, the term refers to alchemists or practitioners of the "Hermetic Art"</note> have succeeded in certain **particulars** <margin>28.</margin> <note>original: *particularibus*; refers to alchemical results that achieve a specific, limited transformation rather than the "Universal" medicine or transmutation</note>, all of this originates from it alone. I confess that I find it quite difficult to grasp how such a thing could be.

### Eudoxus.

This **philosopher** nonetheless explains it clearly enough. He says that several **artists**, who recognized the **Stone** only in an imperfect manner and knew only a part of the work, yet meanwhile—after they had worked upon the **Stone** and found the means to separate its **Spirit**, which contains its **tincture** <term>tincture: the potent, concentrated essence of the stone capable of "dyeing" or transmuting base metals into gold or silver</term> within itself, from it—they brought it so far that they added some of it to the **imperfect metals**, which have an affinity with the **Stone**. ## Pyrophilus.

It is natural to conclude, from what you have said, that there are people who hold the **Stone** <note>original: *Stein*; the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals and curing all diseases</note> in their very hands, and yet do not know all its **virtues** <note>original: *Tugenden*; in early modern science, "virtues" refers to the inherent powers or medicinal properties of a substance</note>; or, even if they already know them, they remain ignorant of how one should work with it so that it might succeed in the **Great Work** <note>original: *grossen Werke*; the completion of the alchemical process to create the Philosopher's Stone</note>, and that this ignorance is the reason they have no progress or benefit at all in their labor. I pray you, tell me, is this not the case?

## Eudoxus.

Without doubt, many **artists** <note>original: *Artisten*; alchemical practitioners</note> have the **Stone** in their possession. Some despise it as a lowly thing; others marvel at it because of the somewhat supernatural signs which it brings with it at its **birth** <note>alchemists often used biological metaphors like "birth" to describe the emergence of a substance during a chemical reaction</note>, and yet they do not know what it is worth. Finally, there are some who well know that it is the true **subject of Philosophy** <term>subjectum Philosophiæ: the raw, underlying matter required to begin the alchemical work</term>; but the labors that the **Sons of Art** <note>original: *Söhne der Kunst*; a traditional term for initiated alchemists</note> must undertake with this noble **subject** are entirely unknown to them, because the books do not teach them, and the **Philosophers** hide the wondrous art which transforms the **Stone** into the **Mercury of the Sages** <term>Mercurium der Weisen: not common quicksilver, but a purified, "philosophical" mercury used as a key solvent in the Great Work</term>, and which provides the instruction on how one...

## Eudoxus.

This source of life-giving water is before the eyes of the whole world, says the **Cosmopolite** <note>original: *Cosmopolita*; a pseudonym for Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist and author</note>, and yet few people recognize it. Gold, silver, metals, and minerals do not contain within themselves an infinitely multiplying tincture; only the **living metals of the Sages** <note>Alchemists distinguished between "dead" metals found in the earth and "living" metals that had been spiritually or chemically activated for the Work</note> have received this multiplying power from Art and Nature. But even so, only those who possess a perfect light in the Philosophical secrets recognize the true origin of the tinctures. You are not among the number of those who do not know from where the Sages draw their treasures without concern that the source might run dry. I have told you clearly, and without confused speech, that the Heavens and the stars—and indeed, primarily the **Sun and Moon** <note>In alchemical symbolism, the Sun and Moon represent Gold and Silver, or the masculine (Sulfur) and feminine (Mercury) principles</note>—are the beginning of this **well of living water**, which alone is capable [of performing] all the wonders that

have. I ask you to tell me whether the magical marriage of Heaven and Earth can be consummated at any time; or whether there are certain seasons of the year, one of which might be more beneficial for celebrating this **Philosophical Wedding** than another?

### Eudoxus.

brought to an end; thus one could also not seek perfection therein, let alone find it. ## Eudoxus.

There is certainly no more fundamental saying than this, for the philosopher <note>original: *Philosophus*</note> indicates to you in this place how the stone must be made, upon which our entire philosophy <note>original: *Philosophia*; here meaning the secret science of alchemy</note> is founded. You have seen how it begins to be born from two bodies <note>original: *Leibern*; the initial metallic or mineral materials</note>; you have seen that it married a heavenly nymph <note>a symbolic term for a volatile, spiritual, or purified substance</note> after its earthly form was taken away from it, so that it might become and make one single thing with her. # Pyrophilus

### Eudoxus.

You would easily find the self-evident meaning of this saying if you remembered that the first and most important effect of the labor of the first work <note>In alchemy, the "First Work" or "First Order" usually refers to the preparation of the "First Matter" or the initial dissolution of the substance.</note> is to turn the body into water—which is our Stone—and that this point is the most hidden of our secrets. I have shown you that this water must be made living and fertile by an astral seed <note>The "astral seed" refers to a celestial influence or "life-force" that alchemists believed descended from the stars to animate physical matter.</note> and by a heavenly spirit, in which the entire power of the Physical Tincture <note>original: *Physischen Tinctur*; a "tincture" is a potent alchemical substance capable of transmuting base metals or healing the body.</note> resides: so much so that, if you pay close attention, you will admit that there is no more certain truth in our philosophy <note>original: *Philosophia*</note> than this which your author <note>Referring to the alchemist Michael Sendivogius.</note> asserts here, namely: that the single thing the wise man needs to accomplish everything is nothing other than the water and the spirit of the body. The water is the body and the soul of our subject <note>original: *Subjecti*; the raw material being worked upon.</note>; the astral seed is its spirit. Therefore, the philosophers assert that their matter <note>original: *Materia*</note> has a body, a soul, and a spirit.
