<meta>This page continues the discussion between Eudoxus and Pyrophilus regarding the power of the Philosopher's Stone to "swallow" or transform metals. Pyrophilus had just questioned whether the Stone could affect perfect metals like gold in the same way it consumes imperfect ones.</meta>

-> * ( o ) * <-

K 4

...perfect metal, which the Stone will surely not be able to swallow.

## Eudoxus.

Your doubt is without merit: for just as the Stone, properly speaking, does not exactly swallow the imperfect metals, but rather changes their nature in such a way that nothing remains of them from which one could recognize what they were before; so too, since the Stone cannot swallow gold, nor transform it into a more perfect metal than it already was, it nonetheless transforms it into a <term>medicine</term> <note>original: *Arzney*; in alchemy, the "Medicine" or "Elixir" refers to the Stone in its final, liquid, or powdered form, capable of healing both metals and the human body</note> a thousand times more perfect than it was before. For it can now transform a thousand times as much imperfect metal according to the degree of <term>over-perfection</term> <note>original: *Ubervollkommenheit*; this refers to the alchemical concept of "multiplication," where the Stone’s power is increased to such an extent that a tiny amount can transform massive quantities of base metal</note> it has received from the <term>Mastery</term> <note>original: *Meisterschaft*; refers to the successful completion of the Great Work, the process of creating the Stone</note>.

## Pyrophilus.

I recognize the weak foundation that my question had: But, to tell you the truth, there is such <term>subtlety</term> <note>original: *subtilität*; the "subtle" language of alchemists was intentionally layered and symbolic to hide secrets from the uninitiated</note> in even the slightest words of the <term>Sages</term> <note>original: *Weisen*; the "Wise Men" or historic masters of the alchemical tradition, such as Hermes or Geber</note>, that it should probably not seem strange to you that I have often been delayed by things that should have been understandable enough to me in themselves.

<summary>Eudoxus explains that the Stone does not destroy gold but elevates it into a potent "Medicine" through the process of multiplication. Pyrophilus reflects on the intentional complexity and hidden meanings found in the writings of the alchemical Sages.</summary>

...is not perhaps that blind **fatality** <note>original: *Fatalität*; a reference to the Stoic or ancient Greek concept of an inescapable, mechanical destiny.</note> of the ancients, nor the supposed chaining or connection, or that necessary and inevitable order of things, which they had to obey, in short, without any distinction: But on the contrary, you are already certain that the **wisdom of GOD** has the upper hand in all things that proceed and happen in the world.

Upon the double foundation which these two reflections and considerations lay and confirm, you cannot doubt that **GOD**—who, in a supreme and sovereign manner, freely manages and governs all goods and gifts—will never permit those who pursue this precious treasure with the intent to use it poorly to ever attain its possession through their labor. And finally, what evil could a perverse mind not cause in the world—having no other aim than to satisfy his insatiable ambition and to quench the thirst of his evil desires—if he had this certain means of putting all his most villainous designs into practice within his power and under his hands? ### A Necessary

# Hermetic Triumph
## to the Reader.

Thus it comes to pass, with these words and conjectures released by this French Philosopher (who may rightly be called **Rich as the burning S*** <note>original Latin: *Dives sicut ardens S***; a common alchemical pseudonym and anagram for Alexandre-Toussaint de Limojon de Saint-Didier, the author of the *Hermetic Triumph*</note>) at the end of his preface, that his explanation of the **War of the Knights** <term>War of the Knights: Referring to the allegorical text *The Ancient War of the Knights* (*Discourse of Eudoxus and Pyrophilus*), which this book interprets.</term> would indeed find its translators. In particular, the Germans <note>original: *Teutschen*</note> should not be the last in this. For, if the French philosophers have a...

<meta>This page continues the translator’s preface. Here, the translator argues that Germans should return the favor by translating those French commentaries back into German.</meta>

* ( o ) *

...the German Philosopher’s **Hermetic Writing** <note>Referring to the alchemical text *The Ancient War of the Knights*, which was originally German.</note> for the use and pleasure of their own countrymen, having sought it out with great effort, had it translated, and provided a splendid **commentary** <note>original Latin: *Commentarium*</note> upon it; so it is only fair that we Germans, in turn, show them the honor—and provide our own countrymen with the benefit and pleasure—of translating this **commentary** and the accompanying epistle into German, as it is truly well worth the effort. In this way, "one hand washes the other." Thus, the inconvenience brought upon the human race by the **Tower of Babel** <note>The biblical story explaining the origin of different languages; the translator views the language barrier as a hindrance to alchemical knowledge.</note>—and especially here upon the **Hermetic students**—is somewhat diminished. I say diminished, not entirely removed. For if the **idioms** <term>idioms: original Latin *idiotismi*, referring to linguistic quirks or figures of speech unique to a specific language</term> and German figures of speech contained in a work written briefly and simply in German are to be expressed in another language according to the **author’s** true meaning, it can hardly happen otherwise: it cannot be done without some constraint, wordiness, or perhaps even errors and mistakes. This is indeed what happened with this **War of the Knights** <note>The allegorical text *The Ancient War of the Knights*.</note>, as shall soon be shown in more detail. And this was precisely the reason why I did not first...

...translate from the **Commentator’s** <note>The author of the French commentary, likely Limojon de Saint-Didier.</note> French version into German, but rather I wished to present the work to the reader in the state in which it was first born <note>Meaning the original German text, before it was translated into other languages.</note>, and, according to the common proverb, to pour the pure water most clearly from the source itself.

Indeed, this **War of the Knights** <note>Referring to the famous alchemical poem *The Ancient War of the Knights* (*Uralter Ritter-Krieg*).</note> is already available in public print without this German translation; nevertheless, I found it necessary to place it before this French **Commentary** <note>original Latin: *Commentario*</note>, so that it would not only be together in one book for the greater convenience of the reader, but also so that every passage explained by this French **Philosopher** <note>original Latin: *Philosopho*</note> might be marked with its specific number in the margin, just as the **Author** had arranged it. For this purpose, I have copied the edition as it was published in **Leipzig in the year 1604** <note>A significant early printing of the text; the translator prioritizes this historical edition for its perceived accuracy.</note>, because as of yet no older version has come to my sight. <meta>This page continues the translator's critique of the existing Latin and French translations of "The Ancient War of the Knights." The previous page began discussing a specific passage (No. 4) where the French translation uses "sur la terre" (on the earth), which the author believes misinterprets the original intent.</meta>

* (o) *

...eternally on the earth <note>original French: *éternellement sur la terre*</note> --- That is: Even if he lay eternally on the ground, or upon the earth. The Latin translator will have written: **in the earth** <note>original Latin: *in terrâ*</note>, which can be understood both as **on the earth** <note>original French: *sur la terre*</note> (as it stands in the **Lord's Prayer** <note>original Latin: *Pater noster*</note>: *as in heaven, so also on earth* <note>original Latin: *sicut in cœlo ita & in terrâ*</note>) as well as **inside the earth** <note>original French: *dans la terre*</note>, and in this way (namely, inside the earth) it should have been rendered.

->2.<-

Under number 9 it stands: **thereupon our Stone answers** --- but in the French, the word (**our**) has been omitted, so that it only stands: *at these words the stone replied* <note>original French: *a ces paroles la pierre répondit*</note>, instead of *our stone replied* <note>original French: *nôtre pierre répondit*</note>. I suspect that the Latin translator was the first to omit it (as he seems to have done consistently), perhaps not knowing that there is a difference between the **Philosophers' Stone** <term>Philosophers' Stone: The legendary substance of alchemy, said to transform base metals into gold and serve as an elixir of life. The translator emphasizes "our stone" to distinguish the specific alchemical substance from common stones.</term> and a stone of common things so named, or as is generally said. The French **Commentator** <note>Likely referring to Limojon de Saint-Didier, who wrote the French commentary on the work.</note>, however, as a discerning **Philosopher** <note>original Latin: *Philosophus*</note>, would surely have kept the word (**our**) there, and perhaps even taken the opportunity to add a learned explanation regarding this distinction, had he found it included in the Latin version.

<summary>The translator critiques errors in the Latin and French translations of the alchemical text, arguing that "in the earth" was mistranslated as "on the earth" and that the vital distinction of "our Stone" was lost.</summary>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's critique of how the term "Stone" is used and translated in alchemical literature. The previous page discussed the omission of the word "our" in French translations, and this page explains the technical distinction between "the stone" and "our stone."</meta>

* (0) *

For when the *Philosophers* <note>original Latin: *Philosophi*</note> say: "Our Stone," they understand it to be in that state where they have it under their labor, in which they strive to bring it to perfection and over-perfection, and to the quality of the so-called Philosophical Stone. 

If, however, the word "Stone" stands alone and without adjectives <note>original Latin: *epitheta*</note>, it can either be applied generally <note>original Latin: *generaliter*</note> to every state of the stone at all times; or it is taken specifically <note>original Latin: *specialiter*</note> (to distinguish it from the Stone of the Wise or its **Matter** <term>Materia: The raw material or fundamental substance used in alchemical operations before it is perfected.</term> <note>original Latin: *Materiæ*</note>), when Nature alone still holds it in her power and has not yet brought it so far that the Artist can come to her aid through a wise imitation. 

But as soon as the *Philosophers* perform their part therein, it is then properly and specifically <note>original Latin: *specialiter*</note> called "their Stone." It is not without cause that the *Philosophers* sometimes intentionally mix these two different considerations of the Stone, but that is done for the sake of the unworthy <note>Alchemists often used "blind" or confusing language to ensure that only those they deemed morally or intellectually "worthy" could understand the secrets of the Great Work.</note>. 

Meanwhile, when one sees that this German *Philosopher* <note>Referring to the original author of the German text being discussed.</note> particularly observes such a consideration <note>original Latin: *consideration*</note> of the Stone, his translator should especially not have hidden such an observation of the **Authentic Author** <note>original Latin: *Authentici Authoris*</note> in Latin from the Hermetic students—

<sig>L 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: hidden,</meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical distinction between "the stone" in its natural state and "our stone," which has been refined by the philosopher's art. The author criticizes translators who fail to maintain this distinction, accusing them of obscuring the original author's intent.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Materia, Hermetic Students, Translation Critique, Alchemical Theory, Our Stone</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of alchemical translations, specifically focusing on the nuance lost when translating the German "Ritter-Krieg" (The War of the Knights) into French. The previous page ended while discussing how the author meticulously observes specific distinctions in terminology.</meta>

* ( 0 ) *

...sidered, or have weighed and held back and forth, so that in accordance with the <term>intention</term> <note>The specific aim or purpose of the writer.</note> of the <term>Author</term> <note>original Latin: *Authoris*</note>, they might take the opportunity to reflect upon the matter and concern themselves with the reason why he sometimes says: "the stone," and at other times "our stone." They should consider what manner of speech he uses when he calls it "the stone" in a simple sense, and what he says, conversely, when he calls it "our stone." Therefore, an error has been made in this: whenever the Stone is introduced as a speaking character in this *War of the Knights* <note>original German: *Ritter-Krieg*; this refers to *The Ancient War of the Knights*, a famous alchemical dialogue where Gold, Mercury, and the Stone of the Philosophers debate their merits.</note>, and the name is placed before the speech or words of the Stone—as is customary in <term>Dialogues</term> <note>original Latin: *Dialogis*</note> and <term>Comedies</term> <note>original Latin: *Comödien*</note>—the <term>authentic</term> <note>original Latin: *authentico*; referring to the original or most reliable version of the text.</note> German version uses the title: **Our Stone.** However, the French translation, instead of "our stone" <note>original French: *nótre pierre*</note>, always says merely: "the stone" <note>original French: *la pierre*</note>.

### 3.

Under Number 10 <note>original Latin: *Sub Num. 10.*</note> stand these words of the Stone: "Dear Gold, it is true, <term>God</term> <note>original German: *GOtt*</note> has granted you honor, beauty, and constancy..." which is given in French as: "it is God who has given you honor, constancy, and beauty" <note>original French: *c'est Dieu, qui t'a donné l'honneur, la constance & de la beauté*</note>—which, according to the ground-text, should be rendered: "who has given you [some] honor," <note>The author is critiquing the specific phrasing of the French partitive article, arguing it shifts the meaning of the original German text.</note>

But the fact that our excellent French Philosopher <note>Likely referring to a specific alchemical commentator or translator of the era</note> added the articles <note>the definite articles "the"</note> is not his fault, but happened *per accidens* <note>Latin: by chance or incidentally</note>. For because he translated the *War of the Knights* <term>Ritter-Krieg: A famous alchemical allegory, "The Ancient War of the Knights," involving a debate between Gold, Mercury, and the Stone</term> not from the German, but from the Latin language; and since the Latin language has no articles; he could not have known—neither in Gold’s objection nor in the Stone’s answer—whether and in which of these two speeches the article was included or omitted in the original language <note>The "Grund-Sprache" or source language, here referring to German</note>. Thus, the article was added in both places by chance. 

However, if the Latin translator had wanted to truly respect the right sense and understanding of the German Philosopher—since it was impossible to achieve this through an article in Latin—he could have managed the Stone’s speech differently and perhaps rendered the words in this manner: *It is true that God has granted you some (or a necessary measure) of honor, beauty, and constancy in the fire.* <note>original Latin: "Verum est, Deum tibi honoris, pulchritudinis & in igne constantiae aliquid (oder necessarium tibi dimensum) concessisse." This phrasing uses "some" or "a measure" to avoid the implication that Gold possesses all honor and beauty.</note>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the linguistic critique of translations of the "Ancient War of the Knights." The previous page ended with a discussion on how errors in translation suggest the "Philosophical Work" is destroyed by the lies of uninitiated practitioners. This page focuses specifically on the word "verderben" (to perish/spoil) and how its grammatical misuse in Latin and French alters the alchemical meaning.</meta>

->* ( 0 ) *<-

Since the Latin translator took the word **perish** <note>German: *verderben*</note> in an active sense—according to his supposed syntax, constructing it with "Gold" as the object of the action—he likely wrote: *they uselessly spend and spoil the gold* <note>original Latin: "aurum inutiliter impendunt & corrumpunt"</note>. This, however, is contrary to the sense and the meaning of the original text.

The French Philosopher <note>Likely referring to the translator of a French edition of the *Ritter-Krieg*</note> clearly saw that something was not right with this passage; therefore, he did not translate it as "they spoil it," but rather that they *strive* to spoil it. For he knows well enough that they will certainly have to leave the gold as it is <note>A reference to the alchemical belief that true gold is a fixed, incorruptible element that cannot be "spoiled" by the clumsy attempts of false alchemists</note>. 

The intention of the original text, however, is that the word **perish** must be understood neutrally—referring not to the Gold, but to the **Sophists** <term>Sophisten: In alchemical literature, this refers to "false alchemists" or "puffers" who lack true philosophical understanding and follow deceptive methods</term>. For "to perish" means *to pass away* or *to go to ruin* <note>original Latin: "perire, interire," etc.</note>. For this reason, it is written with a "d" and actually derives from the word **to suffer want** <note>German: *darben*—to starve or live in extreme poverty</note>; it should, by rights, have been written with an "ä" <note>The author is arguing that *verderben* (to perish) should be spelled *verdärben* to show its etymological link to *darben* (want/poverty)</note>. 

However, "to spoil" or "to corrupt" <note>original Latin: "corrumpere"</note> is written with a "t" <note>The author suggests a distinction between *verderben* (to perish) and *verterben* (to spoil), though this distinction was not standardized in 18th-century German</note>, which does not appear in the German text. The Latin translator should instead have written: *we observe that they achieve nothing through these means, but rather spend their gold uselessly, and thereby failing, they go miserably to ruin and fall into extreme poverty.* <note>original Latin: "animadvertimus, quod hiſce nihil efficiant, ſed potius aurum eorum inutiliter impendant, eoque ipſo deficientes miſe- rè peſſum eant, & in ſummam egeſtatem incidant."</note>

<summary>This page critiques the Latin and French translations of an alchemical allegory, arguing that a grammatical misunderstanding of the German word "verderben" led translators to wrongly claim gold is spoiled, when the text actually means the false alchemists themselves fall into poverty.</summary>

excellent French *Commentator* <note>original: *Commentator*</note> must admit, and consequently had *Pyrophilus* <note>A character name often used in alchemical dialogues, meaning "lover of fire"</note> pose a question regarding this Gordian knot of doubt, asking how it might be resolved? <meta>This page continues the critique of the Latin and French translations of the "Ancient War of the Knights." ->* ( 0 ) *<-

...occurred, are not known. For one sees that the French Philosopher <note>original: *Philosophus*; likely referring to the author of the commentary on "The Ancient War of the Knights"</note> understood the Latin words in this way and sense: He is imperfect, but, precisely for that reason (namely, because he is imperfect) he is not the Mercury of the Sages <term>Mercury of the Sages: *Mercurius Sapientum*; the purified, philosophical mercury used in the Great Work, as opposed to common quicksilver</term>. The German [author], however, understands them thus: He is imperfect, but for that reason it does not necessarily follow that he is the Mercury of the Sages, even though he is imperfect; or, it does not follow from this that he is the Mercury of the Sages. 

Because he [the translator] insisted on using the word *therefore* <note>original Latin: *propterea*</note>, which could just as well have been omitted, he should at the very least have placed the *not* <note>original Latin: *non*</note> at the beginning, such as: *nevertheless, it is not for that reason that he is the Mercury of the Sages* <note>original Latin: *non tamen propterea est Mercurius Sapientum*</note>, or: *it does not, however, follow from this that he is therefore the Mercury of the Sages.* <note>original Latin: *non tamen inde conficitur, eum propterea esse Mercurium Sapientum.*</note> 

Thus, this noble Philosopher may have been deceived by the ambiguity <note>original: *æquivocation*</note> of this Germanism <term>Germanism: *Germanismus*; a phrasing that follows German grammatical logic even when written in another language like Latin, often leading to misunderstandings by non-German speakers</term>. One sees how he must twist and turn in the answer of Eudoxus <note>A character in alchemical dialogues representing "right thinking" or wisdom</note> to clear the German Philosopher of the shame of the supposedly committed contradiction <note>original: *Contradiction*</note>. Whether and how he succeeded in this, and whether the doubt <note>original: *dubium*</note> brought forward by Pyrophilus <note>A character representing the "lover of fire" or a student of alchemy</note> was sufficiently [addressed] through the answer of Eudoxus...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of translation errors in the "Ancient War of the Knights." The previous page explained how a grammatical "Germanism" in a Latin translation led the French commentator, Eudoxus, to misunderstand the relationship between "imperfection" and the "Mercury of the Sages." The author is now addressing whether Eudoxus’s resulting interpretation—his "Philodoxy"—is valid despite the error.</meta>

->* ( 0 ) *<-

**Philodoxy** <note>original catchword: *lofo-* / text: *doxi*. Likely *Philodoxie*, meaning "love of one’s own opinion," used here to describe Eudoxus’s specific interpretation.</note>, as learned and useful as it may be, is resolved, I shall leave it at that. Meanwhile, the matter may have appeared to him as one of the customs of the **Philosophers** <term>Philosophers: In alchemical literature, this refers to the "True Philosophers" or Alchemists who have mastered the Great Work and the secrets of nature.</term>, where they—for the sake of the unworthy and for other reasons—are often accustomed to contradict themselves. The true **students of Hermes** <term>Students of Hermes: *Hermestis-Schüler*; those who follow the teachings of Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical father of alchemy.</term> take no offense at this; rather, they allow it to give them the opportunity to reflect precisely and rationally upon the matter and the cause of such apparent and intentional self-contradiction, and thereby unexpectedly discover one truth or another. 

As the **Cosmopolite** <note>original: *Cosmopolita*; the pseudonym of Michael Sendivogius, a famous 17th-century alchemist.</note> says in the *Epilogue of the Treatises* <note>original: *Epilogo Tractatuum*</note>: *Do not be troubled that sometimes contradictions occur in my treatises, in the usual manner of the Philosophers: Note Well: You have need of them, if you understand. A rose is not found without thorns.* <note>original Latin: *Noli moveri, quod aliquando contradictoria in meis Tractatibus, more Philosophorum usitato, tibi occurrunt: NB. Opus habes illis, si intelligis. ...[which] comes from the Moon, must be tempered <note>balanced or moderated</note>. For Algahel <note>likely referring to a medieval authority or a pseudo-epigraphical work attributed to Al-Ghazali</note> says in *Counsel on the Conjunction of the Mass of the Sun and Moon*: *The Sun* (which our author calls *Vulcan* <term>Vulcan: here referring to the transformative fire used by the alchemist</term>) *is the source of all heat: but the Moon is the mistress of moisture.* <note>original: *Sol... est fons totius caloris : Luna autem domina humiditatis.*</note> And *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus</note>: *Moisture is from the dominion of the Moon: but richness <note>original: *pinguedo*; literally "fatness" or "oiliness," referring to the combustible principle or soul</note> is from the dominion of the Sun.* <note>original: *Humiditas est ex dominio Lunæ : pinguedo autem ex dominio Solis.*</note> Therefore the *Cosmopolite* <note>Michael Sendivogius, a famous 17th-century alchemist</note> says: 

*The internal heat is much more effective than this elemental fire, but it is tempered by earthly water, which from day to day penetrates the pores of the earth and cools it; thus the air, which flies around the world from day to day, tempers the celestial Sun and its heat.* <note>original: *Calor intrinsecus multo efficacior est hoc igne elementali...*</note>

*And elsewhere: Just as the central Sun has its sea and crude, perceptible water; so the celestial Sun also has its sea and subtle and imperceptible water ---* <note>original: *Sicuti Sol centralis habet suum mare & aquam crudam...*</note>

Thus, for the reasons cited, the Vulcan of the Wise <term>the Wise: a common term for accomplished alchemists or "philosophers"</term> must be Lunar. Now we also want to look at the French version, which reads as follows: *by the help of the Lunar Vulcan.* <note>original: *par le secours de Vulcain Lunatique.*</note> The word *Lunatique* means in German "moon-sick" <note>original: *Mohnsüchtig*; an archaic term for somnambulism or being "moon-struck," which the translator here finds confusing in a technical context</note>: although I do not know whether this word might have another and perhaps *more general meaning* <note>original: *generaliorem significationem*</note> in French (which will be best known to the French).

<meta>catchword: Should [Sollte]</meta>

<summary>This page explores the alchemical necessity of balancing "Vulcan" (heat/Sun) with "Lunar" (moisture/Moon) influences, citing authorities like Hermes and the Cosmopolite. It also contains a linguistic discussion on the translation of the French term "Lunatique."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the nature of the alchemical fire, specifically addressing whether it can be characterized as "mad" or "insane" (Wahnsüchtig). The author refers to Paracelsus and the Turba Philosophorum to emphasize that the transformative fire must be controlled and balanced, not a destructive "frenzy."</meta>

->* ( 0 ) *<-

If this were the case, the matter would be beyond **doubt** <note>original: *dubium*</note>. However, if it could be understood in no other way than as **madness** <term>Wahnsucht: literally "delusional craving," referring here to a pathological or unbalanced state of mind</term>, it would not be consistent with the original text. For madness is such a loathsome **condition** <note>original: *affectus*</note> that man—despite the spiritual state in which God placed him—not only yields himself entirely to the course of the world and the stars, becoming like cattle and wild beasts; but he even willfully elevates such a beastly astral drive to a **degree** <note>original: *gradum*</note> and such an **imbalance** <term>Dis-temperament: a lack of proper proportion between the bodily or spiritual qualities, a disharmony of the humors</term> exceeding the ordinary course of nature, that he breaks out into a complete frenzy (as can be seen in the case of Paul in *Acts 9:1* <note>Referencing Saul, before his conversion, who was "breathing out threatenings and slaughter against the disciples of the Lord"</note>). *Theophrastus Paracelsus* <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the famous physician and alchemist</note> speaks very shrewdly of such **mania** <note>original: *Mania*</note> in his beautiful philosophical **treatise, *On Lunatics*** <note>original: *Tractat, de Lunaticis*</note>. Now, the title of the *War of the Knights* <note>referring to *The Ancient War of the Knights* (Der Uralte Ritter-Krieg), a well-known alchemical poem</note> would hardly mean to suggest that the Philosopher’s Stone was prepared by students of nature with the help of such a raging **Vulcan** <term>Vulcan: the god of fire, used here as a metaphor for the heat of the alchemical furnace</term>. Rather, the **Assembly** <note>referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, or "Assembly of the Philosophers," one of the most influential early Latin alchemical texts</note> says: *Do not overheat the bath of Citrinus and his wife, lest they be deprived of sense and motion.* <note>original: *Ne incendatis balneum Citrini & ejus uxoris plurimum, ne sensu & motu priventur.*</note> Also: *Revere the King and his wife and do not burn them with too much fire* — — —

# Epistle to the True Disciples of Hermes, <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary figure credited as the founder of the Hermetic tradition and alchemy.</note>
### containing
## The Six Principal Keys of the Secret Philosophy. <note>original: *Philosophiæ*. In this context, "Philosophy" or "Chemical Philosophy" refers to the study and practice of alchemy.</note>

If I had intended with this epistle to speak in favor of the truth of our philosophy to those who imagine it to be merely an empty thought and a strange adventure, then I would follow the example of many masters experienced in this **Great Art**: <note>The "Great Art" (Ars Magna) is a standard term for the alchemical process of transformation.</note> I would seek to free such minds from their errors, presenting to them the importance and soundness of the principles in our science, which are supported by the laws and effects of nature, and would speak only simply and superficially of its **practice**. <note>original: *Practica*</note> But since my intention is quite different, and I direct this writing of mine only to you, wise disciples of Hermes and true **children of the art**, <note>A traditional term for initiated alchemists who have reached a level of spiritual and technical maturity.</note> my sole purpose is to serve as a guide for you along this track, which is so difficult to find. Our art-work is truly a path in a real sandy soil, where one must look much more toward the **North Star** <note>The North Star (Polaris) symbolizes a fixed point of truth or a specific chemical indicator that guides the alchemist through the "desert" of confusing operations.</note>

<summary>This page introduces an alchemical treatise on the "Six Keys," addressed to dedicated practitioners rather than skeptics. The author frames alchemy as a rigorous science based on natural laws, comparing the difficulty of its practice to navigating a trackless desert by the North Star.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes, Great Art, alchemy, alchemical practice, children of the art, North Star, Hermetic philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 196, the text concludes a comparison between navigating by the "North Star" and following the "footprints" of previous travelers. The author warns that the path of alchemy is cluttered with the confusing tracks of those who have failed or misled others.</meta>

<header>* ( 2 ) *</header>

...one must direct [one's course] by nothing other than the footprints that one sees imprinted. The confusion of the footsteps left behind by an almost infinite number of people is so great, and one finds there so many different incorrect footpaths, almost all of which lead away into monstrous and terrible wildernesses, so that it is almost impossible to reach the right way, which only the **Sages**, <note>original: *Weisen*—the masters of the alchemical craft who have successfully completed the Great Work.</note> to whom Heaven is favorable, have known how to successfully track and recognize.

This muddle holds the **children of the art** <note>original: *Kinder der Kunst*—a traditional term for initiated students or "sons" of the Hermetic tradition.</note> back entirely; some right at the beginning, others in the middle of the *philosophical* <note>original: *Philosophischen*</note> course, and some even when they approach the end of this tedious path and already begin to discover the happy outcome of their undertaking, only to realize that the small part remaining to them of the way will be the very hardest. They do not know that the enviers of their fortune <note>In alchemical literature, "the enviers" (*invidi*) refers to authors who intentionally obscured their writings with riddles or "pits" to prevent the unworthy from succeeding.</note> have hollowed out deep pits and abysses in the middle of the road, and that unless they know the secret bypasses by which the Sages are accustomed to escape such dangerous traps, they will unfortunately lose all the advantages they have obtained with great effort, just when they fully imagined they had finally overcome all difficulties.

I confess to you sincerely that the **practice** <note>original: *Practica*</note> of our art is the most difficult thing in the world, not in view of its **operations**, <note>original: *Operationum*—the specific laboratory procedures and chemical transformations.</note> but in view of the difficulties which exist—

# Epistle to the True
# Disciples of Hermes,

->containing within itself<-

## The Six Most Eminent Keys of
## Secret Philosophy.

If I had intended with this **epistle** <note>original: *Sendschreiben*—a formal letter intended for circulation among a specific group.</note> to advocate for the truth of our **philosophy** <note>original: *Philosophiæ*—in this context, "philosophy" refers specifically to Alchemy, often called the "Secret Philosophy."</note> to those who imagine it to be nothing but a hollow thought and a wondrously strange adventure, then I would follow the example of many masters experienced in this great **Art**; I would seek to free such minds from their errors, presenting them with the importance and solidity of the foundations of our **science** <note>original: *Wissenschaft*—the systematic study of alchemical principles.</note>, which are supported by the laws and workings of nature, and would only speak simply and briefly of its **practice** <note>original: *Practica*—the actual laboratory operations as opposed to the theory.</note>. But since my intention is quite different, and I direct this writing only to you, wise disciples of **Hermes** <note>original: *Hermetis*—referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of the Hermetic tradition and alchemy.</note>, and true children of the Art; my sole purpose is to serve as a **guide** <note>original: *Wegweiser*—literally a "signpost" or "way-shower."</note> for you on this track that is so difficult to find. Our artistic labor is truly a path through a soil of pure sand, where one must [look] much more toward the **North Star**...

## First Key.

The first key is that which opens the dark prisons in which the Sulfur <note>original: *Schwefel*; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of matter.</note> is incarcerated: it is this key that knows how to extract the seed from the body, and which forms the Philosopher’s Stone through the joining of the male and the female, of the spirit and the body, of Sulfur and of Mercury <note>original Latin: *Mercurii*; Mercury represents the fluid, passive, and feminine principle.</note>. Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundation of alchemy is attributed.</note> has clearly and publicly demonstrated the operation of this key with the following words: *That which is hidden from the caverns of the metals is the venerable stone, splendid in color, a sublime mind, and an open sea.* <note>original Latin: "De cavernis Metallorum occultus est, qui lapis est venerabilis, colore splendidus, mens sublimis & mare patens."</note> This stone has a bright radiance; it holds within it a spirit of a high origin; it is the Sea of the Sages, in which they catch their mystery-filled fish. This same philosopher remarks in an even more singular way upon the birth of this wonderful stone, when he says: *The King shall come from the fire and shall rejoice in the marriage, and hidden things shall be made manifest.* <note>original Latin: "Rex ab igne veniet, ac conjugio gaudebit, & occulta patebunt."</note> He is a king crowned with honors, who is born in the fire, who rejoices in the union with the [female partner] assigned to him...

## Second Key.

The second key dissolves the composite or the <term>Stone</term>, and begins the separation of the elements in a philosophical manner. This separation of the elements happens in no other way than through the elevation of the subtle and pure parts over the coarse and earthy ones. Whoever knows how to sublimate <note>to purify a substance by heating it into a vapor and allowing it to solidify again</note> the <term>Stone</term> in a philosophical manner, he can with justice and right bear the name of a Philosopher, because he knows the fire of the Sages, which is the only tool that can effect this sublimation. No philosopher has ever freely and openly revealed this secret fire and this powerful <term>Agent</term> <note>original: "Agens"; the active force or catalyst in the chemical reaction</note>, which effects all the wonders of the art. ## Third Key.

The third key encompasses a longer series and order of *operations* <note>original Latin: *operationibus*</note> within itself than all the others put together. The *Philosophers* <note>original: *Philosophi*; historical term for alchemists who studied the secrets of nature</note> have spoken very little of it, even though the perfection of our *Mercury* <note>not common quicksilver, but the "philosophical mercury" or the essential spirit used in the Great Work</note> *depends* <note>original: *dependiret*</note> upon it. Indeed, even the most sincere among them—such as *Artephius* <note>a celebrated 12th-century alchemist</note>, *Trevisan* <note>original: *Trevisanus*; referring to Bernard of Trevisan</note>, and *Flammel* <note>original: *Flammellus*; Nicholas Flammel, the famous 14th-century French figure associated with the Philosopher's Stone</note>—have passed over the *preparations* <note>original: *præparationes*</note> of our *Mercury* in silence. There is almost not a single one of them who does not simply *presume* <note>original: *supponiret*</note> these longest and most demanding *operations* of our *practice* <note>original: *praxis*</note>, instead of teaching and explaining the longest and most important *operations* of our *practice* <note>original: *practica*</note> as they should have done.

Because I have now made the decision to extend my hand to you on this part of the path that you must walk—where, in the absence of a light, it is impossible to find the right path—I will express myself more openly than the *Philosophers* have done regarding this third key. Or, at the very least, I will take care to maintain the order of everything that they have discussed so confusedly and in such a disordered manner regarding this *subject* <note>original: *Subjecto*</note>, so that without an inspiration from Heaven, or without the assistance of a faithful friend, one must undoubtedly remain stuck in this Daedalian Labyrinth <note>a reference to the inescapable maze of Greek myth; here describing the complexity of alchemical theory</note> without hope of finding one’s way out

### Third Key.

The third key encompasses in itself a longer series and order of <term>operations</term> <note>Latin: *operationibus*; the chemical and spiritual procedures performed by the alchemist</note> than all the others put together. The <term>Philosophers</term> <note>Latin: *Philosophi*; the historic adepts and masters of alchemy</note> have spoken very little about it, despite the fact that the perfection of our <term>Mercury</term> <note>Latin: *Mercurii*; "Philosophical Mercury," the essential spirit or universal solvent of the work, rather than common quicksilver</note> <term>depends</term> <note>Latin: *dependiret*</note> upon it. Indeed, even the most sincere among them—such as Artephius, Trevisan, and Flammel—have passed over the <term>preparations</term> <note>Latin: *præparationes*</note> of our Mercury in silence; and there is almost not a single one of them who does not simply <term>presuppose</term> <note>Latin: *supponiret*; to assume as already known</note> these longest and most demanding <term>operations</term> of our <term>practice</term> <note>Latin: *praxis*</note>, instead of teaching and explaining the longest and most important <term>operations</term> of our <term>practical work</term> <note>Latin: *practica*</note> as they should have.

Since I have now made the decision to extend my hand to you in this part of the path that you must walk—where, in the absence of a lamp, it is impossible to find the right path—I will express myself more openly than the <term>Philosophers</term> have done regarding this third key; or I will at least maintain the order of everything that they have said about this <term>subject</term> <note>Latin: *Subjecto*</note> in such a confused and disorderly manner that, without an inspiration from Heaven or the assistance of a faithful friend, one must undoubtedly remain stuck in this <term>Daedalian</term> <note>original: *Dädalischen*; a reference to the complex labyrinth of Greek myth, signifying an almost inescapable maze</note> labyrinth, without hope of finding a way out...

...know that nothing is so contrary to one another as fire and water; and nonetheless, the wise artist must establish peace among these enemies, who fervently love each other at the bottom. Regarding this, the Cosmopolite <note>original: *Cosmopolita*; the pseudonym of Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous alchemist who wrote about the "Secret Fire"</note> provides a good method in these few words: "Therefore, once the substances are purified, see to it that fire and water become friends; this they will easily do in their own earth, which had ascended with them." <note>original: *Purgatis ergo rebus, fac ut ignis & aqua amici fiant; quod in terrâ suâ, quæ cum iis ascenderat, facile facient.*</note> Therefore, be very observant of this point; soak the earth often with its water, and you will attain what you seek. Must not the body be dissolved <note>original: *dissolviret*</note> by the water, and the earth be permeated by its moisture, if it is to be made fit for birthing? According to the saying of the Sages, the spirit is Eve and the body is Adam; they must be brought together in this way for the reproduction of their kind. Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy</note> says the same thing in other words: "For water is the most powerful nature, which transcends and awakens the nature fixed in the body—that is, it gladdens it." <note>original: *Aqua namque est fortissima natura, quæ transcendit, & fixam corpore naturam excitat, hoc est, lætificat.*</note> In a word, these two substances <note>original: *Substantiæ*</note>, which are entirely of one nature but of two distinct genders, embrace one another with just such love and pleasure as man and wife, and rise up together imperceptibly, leaving only a little dregs <note>original: *Hefen*; the sediment or impurities remaining at the bottom of the flask</note> at the bottom of the vessel; so that the soul, the spirit, and the body, after a precise purification <note>original: *Reinigung*</note> has been completed, finally appear inseparably united under a much nobler and more perfect form than the previous...

...prudence <note>original German: *fichtigkeit*, likely the conclusion of *Vorsichtigkeit* (prudence) or *Aufrichtigkeit* (sincerity)</note>, which one may always draw through the enlightenment of a deep-rooted **philosophy**; the **Compositum Artis** <term>Composition of the Art; the chemical mixture created by the alchemist</term> is not entirely of the same kind in one stage as in the **Third Work**; nonetheless, it is always the Artist who fashions it. **Geber** <note>A legendary figure in alchemy, often identified with the 8th-century Jabir ibn Hayyan, whose writings established the core theories of medieval chemistry</note> defines it as a mixture of **mercury** with **sulfur**—that is, the **volatile** with the **fixed**—which both, while acting upon one another, make each other alternately volatile and fixed until a state of perfect **fixity** <note>The state where a substance is stable and no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to fire</note> is reached.

Consider the example of Nature: You see that the earth would never bring forth fruit if it were not permeated by its moisture, and the moisture would always remain sterile if it were not held and stabilized by the dryness of the earth.

Therefore, you should be certain that no good progress can be made in our Art if, in the first work, you do not purify the **Serpent** <term>A symbol for the raw, chaotic *prima materia* or "First Matter" often associated with the dragon or snake from the mire</term> born from the filth of the earth; if you do not make the dreggy and black sediments white, in order to bring forth the white sulfur, the **Salmiac** <term>Sal Ammoniac; here referring to a purified, crystalline salt of the philosophers</term> of the Sages, which is their **chaste Diana** <term>The Roman goddess of the moon, symbolizing the "Albedo" or whitening stage of the alchemical process</term> as she bathes. ## Fourth Key.

The fourth key of the Art is the beginning of the second work; this transforms our water into earth. There is no water in the world that can be transformed into earth through a simple boiling <note>The original German "schlechte Kochung" uses "schlecht" in its archaic sense meaning "simple" or "plain."</note> except this one alone: For the **Mercury** <term>Mercurius</term> of the Sages carries its own **Sulfur** <term>Schwefel</term> within its center, which **coagulates** <term>coaguliret</term> it. The transformation of the spirit into earth is the only operation in this work: Therefore, cook with patience. 

If you have proceeded well, you will soon see the signs of this **coagulation** <term>Coagulation</term> <note>The process of a liquid thickening or becoming solid.</note>, and if they do not appear at their proper time, they will never appear: For it is an infallible sign that you must have failed in some essential matters in the first operations: For to turn the spirit, which is our **Mercury** <term>Mercurius</term>, into a body, the body in which the **Sulfur** <term>Schwefel</term>—which **coagulates** <term>coaguliret</term> the **Mercury** <term>Mercurius</term>—is enclosed must be well **dissolved** <term>dissolviret</term>. **Hermes** <term>Hermes</term> <note>Refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of the hermetic and alchemical traditions.</note> assures us that our **Mercurial Water** <term>Mercurial-Wasser</term> has taken on all the virtues attributed to it by the Sages once it has been transformed into earth. *Its power is complete if it has been turned into earth.* <note>original Latin: "Vis ejus integra est, si in terram conversa fuerit." This is a famous line from the Emerald Tablet, suggesting that the spiritual energy must be grounded in physical matter to be effective.</note> A wonderful earth because of its fertility: The Earth of Promise of the Sages, or the Promised Land of the

## Fifth Key.

The fifth key of our work is the **fermentation** <term>Fermentation</term> of the stone with its perfect body, in order to make from it the medicine of the third order. I will say nothing of the third work in particular, other than this: that the perfect body is a necessary **leaven** <term>Sauerteig</term> <note>Literally "sourdough." In alchemy, a leaven is a small amount of a substance (like gold or the Stone itself) used to "infect" and transform a larger mass of matter.</note> in our dough; that the spirit [is] the union of the dough with the leaven—

->* ( 0 ) *<-

...must be made, just as water soaks the flour and dissolves the leaven <term>Sauerteig</term> <note>In alchemy, "leaven" refers to a substance (often gold or the previously prepared Stone) added to the base matter to initiate a transformative "fermentation."</note> to make a leavened dough, so that it is fit to bake bread from it. This comparison is quite correct; Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure credited as the founder of alchemy.</note> was the first to use it: "For just as a mass cannot be fermented without a leaven; so when you have sublimated the body, purified it, and separated the filth from the dregs: when you wish to join them, place the leaven in them, and prepare the water and earth so that the mass becomes leaven." <note>original Latin: "Sicut enim massa sine fermento fermentari non potest; sic cum corpus sublimaveris, mundaveris, & turpitudinem à fæce separaveris: cum conjungere volueris, pone in eis fermentum, & aquam terram confice, ut massa fiat fermentum."</note> By taking this opportunity to treat fermentation, the Philosopher <note>Referring to Hermes.</note> here repeats the entire work and demonstrates that, just as the mass of dough becomes entirely leaven through the rising of the ferment <term>Fermenti</term> that has been added to it; so also the entire philosophical composition <term>confection</term>, through this operation, becomes a leaven that is capable of fermenting a new matter <term>Materiam</term> and increasing it infinitely.

   If you carefully observe how bread is baked, you will find the proportion that you must observe between the materials <term>materiis</term> that go into your philosophical dough. Do bakers not take more flour for their dough than leaven, and more water than leaven and flour? The laws of nature are the rules you should follow in the work of our entire mastery <term>Meisterschafft</term>. I have given you all the necessary instruction regarding all the most prominent points, so that it would be a superfluous thing...

# Sixth Key.

The sixth key teaches the multiplication of the Stone <term>Stein</term> through the repetition of the very same previous operation, which consists of nothing else than opening and closing, dissolving <term>dissolviren</term> and coagulating <term>coaguliren</term>, imbibing <term>imbibiren</term> and drying <term>desicciren</term>: whereby the virtues of the Stone increase infinitely without ceasing. Since my undertaking was not to fully describe the practice <term>praxin</term> of the three medicines <note>In this context, "medicines" refers to the different stages of the Stone's perfection (the first, second, and third orders).</note>, but only to instruct you on the most significant operations <term>operationibus</term> concerning the preparation <term>præparation</term> of the Mercury <term>Mercurii</term> <note>The "Mercury of the Philosophers," a vital solvent or essence, not common liquid mercury.</note>, which the Philosophers generally tend to pass over in silence to hide the secrets—which are only for the wise—from the wicked: I will therefore dwell no longer on this point, nor tell you at great length about the Projection <term>Projection</term> <note>The final act of casting the Stone onto base metals to achieve transmutation.</note> of the medicine, for the successful progress you expect does not depend upon it. My instructions have not been extensive, except regarding the third key, because it encompasses a long series and order of operations <term>Operationum</term>, which, though they are simple and natural, nevertheless require a great understanding of the laws, of Nature, and of the properties of our... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the catchword "rer" suggests the next word is "unserer" (our), likely referring to "our matter" or "our stone."</note>

...is: namely that the first <note>The "calcination of the laity" mentioned on the previous page.</note> destroys the body, and consumes and drives away the greatest part of its **radical moisture** <term>radicalischen Feuchtigkeit</term> <note>In alchemy, "radical moisture" refers to the innate, essential life-fluid of a substance that allows it to be flexible and undergo transformation.</note>: But the other <note>Philosophical calcination.</note> not only preserves the aforementioned moisture for the body through its reduction into a lime; but it also increases the same noticeably and very much.

Experience will teach you this great truth in **practice** <term>praxi</term>; for you will indeed realize that this philosophical **calcination** <term>Calcination</term>, which sublimates and distills the **Stone** <term>Stein</term> while calcining it in this way, increases its moisture by a great deal: The cause of this is as follows: because the fiery **Spirit** <term>Geist</term> of the natural fire incorporates itself into those **substances** <term>Substantias</term> with which it has an **analogy** <term>Analogiam</term> and **affinity** <term>Verwandtschafft</term>. Our **Stone** <term>Stein</term> is an astral fire, which has a **sympathy** <term>Sympathiam</term> with the natural fire, and which, like a true **Salamander** <note>A legendary lizard-like creature believed to be able to survive and flourish in fire; a symbol for the "Stone" or the "Spirit" during the heating process.</note>, takes its birth, nourishes itself, and grows within the elemental fire that is **geometrically** <term>geometricè</term> proportioned to it, etc.

->By way of anagram <note>original: *κατ' ἀναγρ.* (kat' anagr.), a Greek abbreviation for "according to the anagram."</note><-

->**RICH AS A BURNING S---** <note>original: *DIVES SICUT ARDENS S---*</note><-

->Name of the Author:<-

->**DYDACUS SENERTUS.**<-

->From the Anagram.<-
